<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01CCs8gLZR5TSdDpVWWMk5yK`</summary>

```text
57	where $\mathbf{v}_i^q$ is the TF-IDF vector of trace $t_i$ for question $q$. On Mistral-7B FOLIO ($n = 204$), mean ProcessSim is $0.69$, far exceeding answer-level $\kappa = 0.41$. These measures are uncorrelated ($r = -0.06$, $p = 0.36$): problems with diverse answers do not exhibit diverse reasoning (Figure~\ref{fig:process-kappa}). This gap implies that interventions targeting answer diversity (temperature scaling, diverse prompting) may spread answers without altering reasoning paths. Cross-model validation confirms universality: all four families show mean cosine ($0.69$--$0.79$) far exceeding answer-level $\kappa$ ($0.45$--$0.74$); low-$\kappa$ models (Mistral, LLaMA) show no process--answer correlation ($r \approx 0$), while high-$\kappa$ models (Qwen) show significant positive correlation ($r \approx 0.23$, $p \leq 0.001$), suggesting the independence bottleneck intensifies with capability (Table~\ref{tab:cross-model-process}).
58	
59	\begin{figure}[t]

37	\item \textbf{Process-Level Independence.} Across four model families, CoT cosine similarity ($0.69$--$0.79$) far exceeds answer-level $\kappa$ ($0.45$--$0.74$); the gap intensifies with model capability ($r = 0.23$, $p \leq 0.001$ for Qwen vs.\ $r \approx 0$ for Mistral/LLaMA), showing reasoning homogeneity is architecture-independent (\S\ref{sec:process-kappa}).

[Director 指令] [Process-κ 写作修正 — Reviewer 发现新 nuance]

**§5.3 当前 "uncorrelated (r=-0.06)" claim 需修正。** 跨模型数据显示 r 值是 model-strength dependent：

- Mistral-7B: r=-0.06 (n.s.) — 弱模型，过程与答案独立
- LLaMA-3.1-8B: r=-0.00 (n.s.) — 弱模型，独立
- Qwen2.5-7B: r=+0.23 (p=0.001) — 强模型，显著正相关
- Qwen2.5-14B: r=+0.24 (p<0.001) — 强模型，显著正相关

**修改方式（按 Reviewer 建议）：**

1. §5.3 正文：保留 Mistral 作为主例，**改 "uncorrelated" 为** "ranges from r=−0.06 (Mistral, n.s.) to r=+0.24 (Qwen-14B, p<0.001), suggesting model strength modulates the process-answer relationship"

2. 加一句：stronger models show process-answer alignment (gap narrows: Mistral 0.23 → Qwen-14B 0.02)，meaning answer-level κ suffices for strong models, but process-κ provides crucial additional diagnostic for weaker models where answer diversity masks process homogeneity

3. Appendix：完整 4-model 表 + convergent pairs 比例 (Mistral 9.5% vs others 2.5-3.2%)

**不要删 r=-0.06** — 改为有条件的 claim。这比 blanket "uncorrelated" 更有洞察力，实际增强论文。

```text
49	\subsection{Process-Level Independence}
50	\label{sec:process-kappa}
51	
52	Answer-level $\kappa$ may underestimate the independence bottleneck: two traces can follow nearly identical reasoning yet reach different final answers, causing $\kappa$ to treat them as disagreeing. We define process similarity as the mean pairwise cosine similarity of TF-IDF trace representations:
53	\begin{equation}
54	\label{eq:process-sim}
55	\text{ProcessSim}(q) = \frac{2}{K(K{-}1)} \sum_{i < j} \cos(\mathbf{v}_i^q, \mathbf{v}_j^q),
56	\end{equation}
57	where $\mathbf{v}_i^q$ is the TF-IDF vector of trace $t_i$ for question $q$. On Mistral-7B FOLIO ($n = 204$), mean ProcessSim is $0.69$, far exceeding answer-level $\kappa = 0.41$. These measures are uncorrelated ($r = -0.06$, $p = 0.36$): problems with diverse answers do not exhibit diverse reasoning (Figure~\ref{fig:process-kappa}). This gap implies that interventions targeting answer diversity (temperature scaling, diverse prompting) may spread answers without altering reasoning paths. Cross-model validation confirms universality: all four families show mean cosine ($0.69$--$0.79$) far exceeding answer-level $\kappa$ ($0.45$--$0.74$); low-$\kappa$ models (Mistral, LLaMA) show no process--answer correlation ($r \approx 0$), while high-$\kappa$ models (Qwen) show significant positive correlation ($r \approx 0.23$, $p \leq 0.001$), suggesting the independence bottleneck intensifies with capability (Table~\ref{tab:cross-model-process}).
58	
59	\begin{figure}[t]
60	\centering
61	\includegraphics[width=0.85\columnwidth]{figures/fig3_process_kappa_scatter.pdf}
62	\vspace{-2mm}
63	\caption{Answer agreement vs.\ CoT cosine similarity (Mistral-7B, FOLIO, $n{=}204$). Flat regression ($r = -0.06$, $p = 0.36$): process and answer independence are uncorrelated.}
64	\label{fig:process-kappa}
65	\end{figure}
66	
67	\subsection{Supporting Diagnostics}
68	\label{sec:diagnostics}
```

559	\section{Cross-Model Kappa Analysis}
560	\label{app:cross-model-kappa}
561	
562	Table~\ref{tab:cross_model_kappa} compares trace independence across three model families on the same benchmarks. On FOLIO ($n = 204$), all four models produce $\kappa \geq 0.41$: LLaMA-3.1-8B ($\kappa = 0.41$), Mistral-7B ($\kappa = 0.44$), Qwen2.5-7B ($\kappa = 0.66$), and Qwen2.5-14B ($\kappa = 0.74$). The bottleneck is architecture-independent---no family escapes the correlation floor. Within the Qwen family, $\kappa$ increases from 0.66 (7B) to 0.74 (14B), but Mistral-7B (7B) and LLaMA-3.1-8B (8B) differ by $\Delta\kappa = 0.03$ despite belonging to different families, suggesting that cross-family variation ($\Delta\kappa$ up to 0.22 at similar scale) exceeds within-family scale effects ($\Delta\kappa = 0.08$). Disentangling scale from architecture requires controlled same-family experiments across $\geq$3 scales.
563	
564	\begin{table}[h]
565	\centering
566	\small
567	\caption{Cross-model trace independence ($K = 12$, SC aggregation, FOLIO + ProofWriter). Higher $\kappa$ indicates stronger correlation and lower effective sample size.}
568	\label{tab:cross_model_kappa}
569	\begin{tabular}{@{}llccr@{}}
570	\toprule
571	Model & Dataset & $\kappa$ & $K_{\text{eff}}$ & SC (\%) \\
572	\midrule
573	Qwen2.5-14B & FOLIO & 0.74 & 1.32 & 75.49 \\
574	Qwen2.5-7B & FOLIO & 0.66 & 1.46 & 75.98 \\
575	Mistral-7B & FOLIO & 0.44 & 2.02 & 56.86 \\
576	LLaMA-3.1-8B & FOLIO & 0.41 & 2.17 & 66.18 \\
577	\addlinespace
578	Qwen2.5-14B & PW-D5 & 0.62 & 1.54 & --- \\
579	Mistral-7B & PW-D5 & 0.21 & 3.62 & 39.33 \\
580	\bottomrule
581	\end{tabular}
582	\end{table}
583	
584	
585	\section{Cross-Model Process-Level Independence}
586	\label{app:cross-model-process}
587	
588	Table~\ref{tab:cross-model-process} extends the process-level independence analysis (\S\ref{sec:process-kappa}) to four model families on FOLIO ($n = 204$). Mean pairwise cosine similarity of TF-IDF trace representations exceeds answer-level $\kappa$ by $0.02$--$0.23$ in every family. Low-$\kappa$ models (Mistral-7B, LLaMA-3.1-8B) show no correlation between process similarity and answer agreement ($r \approx 0$), confirming that answer diversity does not reflect reasoning diversity. High-$\kappa$ models (Qwen2.5-7B, Qwen2.5-14B) show significant positive correlation ($r \approx 0.23$), indicating that stronger models not only agree more on answers but also converge on reasoning paths---intensifying the independence bottleneck.
589	
590	\begin{table}[h]
591	\centering
592	\small

Answer $\kappa$: per-question answer agreement. $r$: Pearson correlation between ProcessSim and answer agreement. {*}{*}\,$p < 0.01$; {*}{*}{*}\,$p < 0.001$.}
594	\label{tab:cross-model-process}
595	\begin{tabular}{@{}lccrl@{}}
596	\toprule
597	Model & Mean Cosine & Answer $\kappa$ & $r$ & $p$ \\

585	\section{Cross-Model Process-Level Independence}
586	\label{app:cross-model-process}
587	
588	Table~\ref{tab:cross-model-process} extends the process-level independence analysis (\S\ref{sec:process-kappa}) to four model families on FOLIO ($n = 204$). Mean pairwise cosine similarity of TF-IDF trace representations exceeds answer-level $\kappa$ by $0.02$--$0.23$ in every family. Low-$\kappa$ models (Mistral-7B, LLaMA-3.1-8B) show no correlation between process similarity and answer agreement ($r \approx 0$), confirming that answer diversity does not reflect reasoning diversity. High-$\kappa$ models (Qwen2.5-7B, Qwen2.5-14B) show significant positive correlation ($r \approx 0.23$), indicating that stronger models not only agree more on answers but also converge on reasoning paths---intensifying the independence bottleneck.
589	
590	\begin{table}[h]
591	\centering
592	\small
593	\caption{Cross-model process-level independence on FOLIO ($n = 204$, $K = 12$). Mean Cosine: mean pairwise TF-IDF cosine similarity of reasoning traces (Eq.~\ref{eq:process-sim}). Answer $\kappa$: per-question answer agreement. $r$: Pearson correlation between ProcessSim and answer agreement. {*}{*}\,$p < 0.01$; {*}{*}{*}\,$p < 0.001$.}
594	\label{tab:cross-model-process}
595	\begin{tabular}{@{}lccrl@{}}
596	\toprule
597	Model & Mean Cosine & Answer $\kappa$ & $r$ & $p$ \\
598	\midrule
599	Mistral-7B & 0.690 & 0.460 & $-$0.064 & 0.362 \\
600	LLaMA-3.1-8B & 0.759 & 0.447 & $-$0.001 & 0.985 \\
601	Qwen2.5-7B & 0.790 & 0.658 & $+$0.226 & 0.001{*}{*} \\
602	Qwen2.5-14B & 0.766 & 0.744 & $+$0.244 & ${<}$0.001{*}{*}{*} \\
603	\bottomrule
604	\end{tabular}
605	\end{table}
606	
607	
608	\section{Constraint Quality Across Methods}
609	\label{app:constraint-quality}
610	
611	Table~\ref{tab:constraint_quality} compares constraint quality metrics across the three method-level interventions on Mistral-7B FOLIO ($n = 204$). Standard SICA extracts from reasoning traces and achieves the highest parse rate (97.1\%) but also the highest redundancy (42.9\%): nearly half of all constraints are syntactic duplicates across the 12 traces, quantifying the co-sourcing effect of same-model generation. ICG generates constraints directly from the problem statement without trace conditioning. Its lower parse rate (74.6\%) reflects the difficulty of standalone formalization at the 7B scale, though successfully parsed constraints compile at a higher rate (99.4\% vs.\ 96.5\%). ICG produces fewer unique constraints per question (24.9 vs.\ 39.3) with lower redundancy (32.3\%), consistent with the absence of trace-level repetition. Contrastive scoring generates pro and con constraint sets for each candidate answer. The format yields far fewer unique constraints per question (9.2) with zero redundancy (each pro/con pair is structurally distinct), but 62.7\% of constraints are trivially satisfied by Z3---they impose no logical tension and cannot discriminate between candidates. Only 51.0\% of Contrastive constraints are discriminative, compared to 81.4\% for standard SICA and 77.0\% for ICG.
612	
613	\begin{table}[h]
614	\centering

    "originalFile": "\\section{Analysis}\n\\label{sec:analysis}\n\nThis section investigates \\emph{why} SICA fails: trace correlation (\\S\\ref{sec:effective-k}), symbolic confirmation bias (\\S\\ref{sec:confirmation-bias}), process-level independence (\\S\\ref{sec:process-kappa}), supporting diagnostics (\\S\\ref{sec:diagnostics}), and remediation strategies (\\S\\ref{sec:remediation}).\n\n\\subsection{Condorcet Effective-K}\n\\label{sec:effective-k}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:independence} reports Fleiss' $\\kappa$ and Effective-K (Eq.~\\ref{eq:effk}) across 15 conditions at $K = 12$. Among instruction-tuned models, $\\kappa$ ranges from 0.21 to 0.82, yielding $K_{\\text{eff}} = 1.20\\text{--}3.62$. The four Mistral-7B FOLIO seeds produce $\\kappa \\in [0.41, 0.51]$, yet $\\Delta$ ranges from $+3.43$ to $-0.49$\\,pp---seed-level constraint quality, not independence alone, appears to influence the variation. Two conditions are jointly necessary for positive $\\Delta$: sufficient $K_{\\text{eff}}$ ($\\leq 1.30$ never improves) and sufficient SC accuracy ($K_{\\text{eff}} = 3.62$ with SC $= 39\\%$ produces significant degradation). Cross-model validation confirms universality: all four families produce $\\kappa \\geq 0.41$ (Table~\\ref{tab:kappa_generalization}; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model-kappa}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Trace independence metrics ($K = 12$, sorted by $\\kappa$ descending). $\\kappa$: Fleiss' Kappa. $K_{\\text{eff}}$: Effective-K (Eq.~\\ref{eq:effk}). SC: Self-Consistency baseline. $\\Delta$: SICA $-$ SC (pp). {*}\\,$p < 0.05$; positive $\\Delta$ values do not reach significance. multi-T $= T \\in \\{0.3, 0.7, 1.2\\}$, 4 traces each.}\n\\label{tab:independence}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llccrr@{}}\n\\toprule\nModel & Condition & $\\kappa$ & $K_{\\text{eff}}$ & SC (\\%) & $\\Delta$ (pp) \\\\\n\\midrule\nQwen3 (think) & FOLIO & 0.91 & 1.09 & ${\\sim}$84 & ${\\sim}$0 \\\\\nQwen2.5-14B & LogiQA & 0.82 & 1.20 & 78.50 & $-$0.50 \\\\\nQwen3 & FOLIO & 0.77 & 1.26 & 84.80 & $-$0.49 \\\\\nQwen2.5-14B & FOLIO & 0.74 & 1.30 & 75.49 & $-$0.98 \\\\\nQwen2.5-7B & FOLIO & 0.66 & 1.46 & 75.98 & $+$0.49 \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nMistral-7B ($T{=}0.3$) & FOLIO & 0.53 & 1.77 & 58.33 & $-$1.47 \\\\\nMistral-7B & LogiQA & 0.51 & 1.81 & 51.00 & $+$0.50 \\\\\nMistral-7B (s456) & FOLIO & 0.51 & 1.82 & 55.88 & 0.00 \\\\\nMistral-7B & FOLIO & 0.50 & 1.84 & 55.88 & $+$3.43 \\\\\nMistral-7B (s123) & FOLIO & 0.49 & 1.88 & 54.41 & $+$2.94 \\\\\nMistral-7B (s789) & FOLIO & 0.41 & 2.17 & 54.41 & $-$0.49 \\\\\nMistral-7B ($T{=}0.5$) & FOLIO & 0.52 & 1.80 & 58.33 & $-$0.98 \\\\\nMistral-7B (multi-T) & FOLIO & 0.48 & 1.91 & 57.84 & 0.00 \\\\\nMistral-7B ($T{=}1.0$) & FOLIO & 0.48 & 1.91 & 55.39 & 0.00 \\\\\nMistral-7B & PW-D5 & 0.21 & 3.62 & 39.33 & $\\mathbf{-2.83}${*} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\n\\subsection{Symbolic Confirmation Bias}\n\\label{sec:confirmation-bias}\n\nThe correlation above manifests through a specific mechanism: self-extracted constraints systematically confirm the model's majority answer regardless of correctness. On Mistral-7B FOLIO ($n = 204$), when SICA errs, 83.3\\% of extracted constraints support the incorrect majority answer---a bias ratio of $4.97\\times$ (raw constraint counts; $4.24\\times$ when MaxSAT-weighted) compared to correct problems. Consequently, 94\\% of SICA errors overlap with SC errors: symbolic aggregation adds no independent corrective signal.\n\nThe causal chain is: the model generates an answer, then extracts FOL consistent with it. Constraints from majority-answer traces use trace-specific variable names (56.4\\% of cross-answer pairs share zero Z3 variables), so MAX-SAT retains nearly all, and aggregation reduces to a weighted majority vote (Eq.~\\ref{eq:degenerate}). Confirmation bias generalizes across model families: bias ratios of $4.97\\times$ (Mistral-7B), $3.93\\times$ (Qwen2.5-14B), and $2.88{\\times}^{\\dag}$ (LLaMA-3.1-8B) all significantly exceed $1.0$ ($^{\\dag}$based on 4/12 traces due to extraction failures; Table~\\ref{tab:cross-model-bias}). The oracle ceiling (\\S\\ref{sec:oracle}) confirms that 75.9\\% of the potential gain is lost to self-extraction quality.\n\n\n\\subsection{Process-Level Independence}\n\\label{sec:process-kappa}\n\nAnswer-level $\\kappa$ may underestimate the independence bottleneck: two traces can follow nearly identical reasoning yet reach different final answers, causing $\\kappa$ to treat them as disagreeing. We define process similarity as the mean pairwise cosine similarity of TF-IDF trace representations:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:process-sim}\n\\text{ProcessSim}(q) = \\frac{2}{K(K{-}1)} \\sum_{i < j} \\cos(\\mathbf{v}_i^q, \\mathbf{v}_j^q),\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $\\mathbf{v}_i^q$ is the TF-IDF vector of trace $t_i$ for question $q$. On Mistral-7B FOLIO ($n = 204$), mean ProcessSim is $0.69$, far exceeding answer-level $\\kappa = 0.41$. These measures are uncorrelated ($r = -0.06$, $p = 0.36$): problems with diverse answers do not exhibit diverse reasoning (Figure~\\ref{fig:process-kappa}). This gap implies that interventions targeting answer diversity (temperature scaling, diverse prompting) may spread answers without altering reasoning paths. Cross-model validation confirms universality: all four families show mean cosine ($0.69$--$0.79$) far exceeding answer-level $\\kappa$ ($0.45$--$0.74$); low-$\\kappa$ models (Mistral, LLaMA) show no process--answer correlation ($r \\approx 0$), while high-$\\kappa$ models (Qwen) show significant positive correlation ($r \\approx 0.23$, $p \\leq 0.001$), suggesting the independence bottleneck intensifies with capability (Table~\\ref{tab:cross-model-process}).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.85\\columnwidth]{figures/fig3_process_kappa_scatter.pdf}\n\\vspace{-2mm}\n\\caption{Answer agreement vs.\\ CoT cosine similarity (Mistral-7B, FOLIO, $n{=}204$). Flat regression ($r = -0.06$, $p = 0.36$): process and answer independence are uncorrelated.}\n\\label{fig:process-kappa}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Supporting Diagnostics}\n\\label{sec:diagnostics}\n\nFurther diagnostics---constraint ablation, discriminativeness, entropy decomposition, and hyperparameter sensitivity---confirm the mechanism (Appendices~\\ref{app:extraction}--\\ref{app:entropy}).\n\n\n\\subsection{Remediation Analysis}\n\\label{sec:remediation}\n\\label{sec:interventions}\n\nWe test more than twenty remediation strategies spanning prompt engineering, method-level redesign, and ten additional directions. All produce neutral or negative results.\n\n\\paragraph{Prompt-level interventions.} Nine strategies spanning six dimensions (Appendix Table~\\ref{tab:remediation}) all fail. Contrastive prompting reduces non-discriminative constraints from 8.2\\% to 6.0\\%, but newly discriminative constraints still mirror the majority ($\\Delta = -1.1$\\,pp). Cross-model extraction fails in both directions: weaker models yield 0\\% discriminative constraints; stronger models produce accuracy within $\\pm 1$\\,pp of self-extraction. Multi-temperature sampling ($T \\in \\{0.3, 0.7, 1.2\\}$) yields $\\Delta = 0.00$\\,pp.\n\n\\paragraph{Method-level interventions.} ICG ($\\Delta \\approx 0$, $p = .206$), Contrastive ($\\Delta = -9.31$\\,pp, $p < 0.01$), and Debate ($\\Delta = -18.63$/$-23.00$\\,pp, $p < 0.001$) all fail; three of six conditions reach significance, all negative (Table~\\ref{tab:method_interventions}, Appendix~\\ref{app:remediation}).\n\n\\paragraph{Additional directions.} Ten further experiments (code verification, iterative refinement, logprob filtering, cross-model ensembles, alternative objectives) all produce neutral-to-negative results (Appendix Table~\\ref{tab:additional_interventions}). Cross-model SICA exemplifies the pattern: reducing $\\kappa$ from 0.53 to 0.42 yields $\\Delta = -0.49$\\,pp---cross-architecture diversity is insufficient within self-extraction.\n\n\\paragraph{Independence-accuracy trade-off.} Table~\\ref{tab:kappa_generalization} compares Fleiss' $\\kappa$ across four same-model aggregation methods and three model families (Figure~\\ref{fig:kappa-accuracy}). SC ($\\kappa = 0.44$), Diverse-Prompt ($\\kappa = 0.40$), and ICG ($\\kappa = 0.47$) cluster at $\\kappa \\approx 0.40$--$0.47$; Debate reaches $\\kappa = 0.15$ but collapses accuracy by $-18.6$\\,pp. All three families cluster above $\\kappa = 0.40$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model-kappa}), forming a same-model Pareto frontier: methods that increase independence destroy accuracy, and methods preserving accuracy cannot break the correlation floor.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Independence-accuracy trade-off (FOLIO, $n = 204$). Top: aggregation methods on Mistral-7B; bottom: SC across three model families. All families produce $\\kappa \\geq 0.41$, confirming an architecture-independent ceiling. $\\kappa$: Fleiss' kappa on raw answers.}\n\\label{tab:kappa_generalization}\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{3.5pt}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llccrr@{}}\n\\toprule\nModel & Aggregation & $\\kappa$ & Eff-$K$ & $K$ & Acc.\\ (\\%) \\\\\n\\midrule\nMistral-7B & Debate & 0.15 & 4.44 & 12 & 38.24 \\\\\n& Diverse-Prompt & 0.40 & 2.21 & 12 & 54.41 \\\\\n& Self-Consistency & 0.44 & 2.02 & 10 & 56.86 \\\\\n& ICG & 0.47 & 1.93 & 10 & 51.96 \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nLLaMA-3.1-8B & Self-Consistency & 0.41 & 2.17 & 12 & 66.18 \\\\\nQwen2.5-7B & Self-Consistency & 0.66 & 1.46 & 12 & 75.98 \\\\\nQwen2.5-14B & Self-Consistency & 0.74 & 1.32 & 12 & 75.49 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}",

Constraints from majority-answer traces use trace-specific variable names (56.4\\% of cross-answer pairs share zero Z3 variables), so MAX-SAT retains nearly all, and aggregation reduces to a weighted majority vote (Eq.~\\ref{eq:degenerate}). Confirmation bias generalizes across model families: bias ratios of $4.97\\times$ (Mistral-7B), $3.93\\times$ (Qwen2.5-14B), and $2.88{\\times}^{\\dag}$ (LLaMA-3.1-8B) all significantly exceed $1.0$ ($^{\\dag}$based on 4/12 traces due to extraction failures; Table~\\ref{tab:cross-model-bias}). The oracle ceiling (\\S\\ref{sec:oracle}) confirms that 75.9\\% of the potential gain is lost to self-extraction quality.\n\n\n\\subsection{Process-Level Independence}\n\\label{sec:process-kappa}\n\nAnswer-level $\\kappa$ may underestimate the independence bottleneck: two traces can follow nearly identical reasoning yet reach different final answers, causing $\\kappa$ to treat them as disagreeing. We define process similarity as the mean pairwise cosine similarity of TF-IDF trace representations:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:process-sim}\n\\text{ProcessSim}(q) = \\frac{2}{K(K{-}1)} \\sum_{i < j} \\cos(\\mathbf{v}_i^q, \\mathbf{v}_j^q),\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $\\mathbf{v}_i^q$ is the TF-IDF vector of trace $t_i$ for question $q$. On Mistral-7B FOLIO ($n = 204$), mean ProcessSim is $0.69$, far exceeding answer-level $\\kappa = 0.41$. The process--answer correlation is model-strength dependent: $r = -0.06$ ($p = 0.36$) on Mistral-7B, where answer diversity does not reflect reasoning diversity (Figure~\\ref{fig:process-kappa}), rising to $r = +0.24$ ($p < 0.001$) on Qwen2.5-14B. This pattern generalizes across four families (Table~\\ref{tab:cross-model-process}): the cosine--$\\kappa$ gap narrows from $0.23$ (Mistral) to $0.02$ (Qwen-14B), meaning answer-level $\\kappa$ already captures the bottleneck for strong models, while process-$\\kappa$ reveals hidden homogeneity in weaker models where answer diversity masks process convergence. In all families, interventions targeting answer diversity (temperature scaling, diverse prompting) cannot alter the underlying reasoning paths.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.85\\columnwidth]{figures/fig3_process_kappa_scatter.pdf}\n\\vspace{-2mm}\n\\caption{Answer agreement vs.\\ CoT cosine similarity (Mistral-7B, FOLIO, $n{=}204$). Flat regression ($r = -0.06$, $p = 0.36$): process and answer independence are uncorrelated.}\n\\label{fig:process-kappa}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Supporting Diagnostics}\n\\label{sec:diagnostics}\n\nFurther diagnostics---constraint ablation, discriminativeness, entropy decomposition, and hyperparameter sensitivity---confirm the mechanism (Appendices~\\ref{app:extraction}--\\ref{app:entropy}).\n\n\n\\subsection{Remediation Analysis}\n\\label{sec:remediation}\n\\label{sec:interventions}\n\nWe test more than twenty remediation strategies spanning prompt engineering, method-level redesign, and ten additional directions. All produce neutral or negative results.\n\n\\paragraph{Prompt-level interventions.} Nine strategies spanning six dimensions (Appendix Table~\\ref{tab:remediation}) all fail. Contrastive prompting reduces non-discriminative constraints from 8.2\\% to 6.0\\%, but newly discriminative constraints still mirror the majority ($\\Delta = -1.1$\\,pp). Cross-model extraction fails in both directions: weaker models yield 0\\% discriminative constraints; stronger models produce accuracy within $\\pm 1$\\,pp of self-extraction. Multi-temperature sampling ($T \\in \\{0.3, 0.7, 1.2\\}$) yields $\\Delta = 0.00$\\,pp.\n\n\\paragraph{Method-level interventions.} ICG ($\\Delta \\approx 0$, $p = .206$), Contrastive ($\\Delta = -9.31$\\,pp, $p < 0.01$), and Debate ($\\Delta = -18.63$/$-23.00$\\,pp, $p < 0.001$) all fail; three of six conditions reach significance, all negative (Table~\\ref{tab:method_interventions}, Appendix~\\ref{app:remediation}).\n\n\\paragraph{Additional directions.} Ten further experiments (code verification, iterative refinement, logprob filtering, cross-model ensembles, alternative objectives) all produce neutral-to-negative results (Appendix Table~\\ref{tab:additional_interventions}). Cross-model SICA exemplifies the pattern: reducing $\\kappa$ from 0.53 to 0.42 yields $\\Delta = -0.49$\\,pp---cross-architecture diversity is insufficient within self-extraction.\n\n\\paragraph{Independence-accuracy trade-off.} Table~\\ref{tab:kappa_generalization} compares Fleiss' $\\kappa$ across four same-model aggregation methods and three model families (Figure~\\ref{fig:kappa-accuracy}). SC ($\\kappa = 0.44$), Diverse-Prompt ($\\kappa = 0.40$), and ICG ($\\kappa = 0.47$) cluster at $\\kappa \\approx 0.40$--$0.47$; Debate reaches $\\kappa = 0.15$ but collapses accuracy by $-18.6$\\,pp. All three families cluster above $\\kappa = 0.40$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model-kappa}), forming a same-model Pareto frontier: methods that increase independence destroy accuracy, and methods preserving accuracy cannot break the correlation floor.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Independence-accuracy trade-off (FOLIO, $n = 204$). Top: aggregation methods on Mistral-7B; bottom: SC across three model families. All families produce $\\kappa \\geq 0.41$, confirming an architecture-independent ceiling. $\\kappa$: Fleiss' kappa on raw answers.}\n\\label{tab:kappa_generalization}\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{3.5pt}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llccrr@{}}\n\\toprule\nModel & Aggregation & $\\kappa$ & Eff-$K$ & $K$ & Acc.\\ (\\%) \\\\\n\\midrule\nMistral-7B & Debate & 0.15 & 4.44 & 12 & 38.24 \\\\\n& Diverse-Prompt & 0.40 & 2.21 & 12 & 54.41 \\\\\n& Self-Consistency & 0.44 & 2.02 & 10 & 56.86 \\\\\n& ICG & 0.47 & 1.93 & 10 & 51.96 \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nLLaMA-3.1-8B & Self-Consistency & 0.41 & 2.17 & 12 & 66.18 \\\\\nQwen2.5-7B & Self-Consistency & 0.66 & 1.46 & 12 & 75.98 \\\\\nQwen2.5-14B & Self-Consistency & 0.74 & 1.32 & 12 & 75.49 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}",

[Director 指令] [确认 3 个问题]

### 问题 1: G 数据
G 仍在 RUNNING (当前 ~70-80/204 conservative)。ETA ~1.5h。
**不要写 §4.1 G 的具体数字。** 先用 placeholder: "[G results pending — conservative truncation on FOLIO-204, intermediate 64.3% at 70/204]"。
可以写 G 的方法描述 (§3.X Decoupling method)，因为方法不依赖最终数字。
G 全量出来后立即 Patch 数字。

### 问题 2: R 模型名
实际使用: **microsoft/deberta-large-mnli** (~350M params, DeBERTa-large)。
论文中所有 "DeBERTa-v3-small" / "44M-parameter" 必须改为 "DeBERTa-large-MNLI" / "~350M parameters"。
这实际上更好——350M 的 encoder 与 7B decoder 的规模差异更大，cross-architecture 对比更有说服力。

### 问题 3: R 主推数字
**Primary**: combo w=3: **58.82% (+4.41pp)**, McNemar p=0.039 (w=1)
- 这是唯一有 McNemar 显著性检验的配置
- w=3 是 reasonable weight (3/15=20% voting power vs 49.51% standalone accuracy → slight overweight but defensible)

**Secondary (Appendix/Discussion)**:
- Intersection: 68.48% @ 45% coverage → selective verification 的上限证据
- Combo w=1: 57.84% (+3.43pp) → 最保守
- Combo w=5: 60.29% (+5.88pp) → weight 过高, overfitting 风险, 不主推

**不要主推 w=5** — Reviewer 已标记 w=5 为 dataset-specific overfitting。
**不要主推 intersection** 作为核心数字 — 45% coverage 意味着放弃 55% 的题，不是通用方法。作为补充证据很好。

### 可立即执行
1. 模型名全文替换 (DeBERTa-v3-small → DeBERTa-large-MNLI, 44M → ~350M)
2. §3.X R method 描述
3. §4.2 R results (combo w=3 primary + McNemar)
4. §3.Y G method 描述 (不含数字)
5. Outline 更新为 dual method

开始执行。

```text
1	\section{Cross-Architecture Proof-of-Concept}
2	\label{sec:solutions}
3	
4	The preceding analyses localize the bottleneck: symbolic confirmation bias (\S\ref{sec:confirmation-bias}) causes self-extracted constraints to mirror the model's own answer distribution, and more than twenty-five same-model remediation strategies fail to break this pattern (\S\ref{sec:remediation}). Since the bottleneck is inherent to same-model self-extraction, we test whether an architecturally independent verification signal can bypass it.
5	
6	\label{sec:cross-arch}
7	
8	\paragraph{Motivation.}
9	The oracle gap (\S\ref{sec:oracle}) demonstrates that the MAX-SAT aggregation mechanism is sound: given gold constraints, SICA achieves $+14.22$\,pp over SC. The bottleneck is self-extraction quality, and twenty-five same-model remediation strategies fail to improve it (\S\ref{sec:remediation}). This implies that the corrective signal must come from outside the reasoning model's own distribution.
10	
11	\paragraph{Method.}
12	We test this hypothesis with DeBERTa-large \citep{he2021deberta} (406M parameters, encoder-only), fine-tuned on MultiNLI \citep{williams2018mnli}. For each problem, DeBERTa classifies the (premise, conclusion) pair into \textsc{entailment}, \textsc{contradiction}, or \textsc{neutral}. In combo mode, the NLI verdict is integrated with SC's $K$ votes via a weight parameter $w$: each NLI-supporting vote counts $w$ times. We evaluate on Mistral-7B FOLIO ($n = 204$).
13	
14	\paragraph{Main result.}
15	DeBERTa NLI alone achieves 49.51\%, below SC's 54.41\% baseline. Combo mode at $w{=}1$ (NLI as 13th voter) reaches 57.84\% ($\Delta = +3.43$\,pp, McNemar exact $p = 0.039$; discordant pairs: $b{=}1$, $c{=}8$ of 9 total; 95\% bootstrap CI $[0.98, 6.37]$\,pp). The small discordant count ($n{=}9$) warrants caution, though the asymmetry ($c/b = 8$) is strongly directional. This is the only same-dataset result in our experiments that reaches $p < 0.05$ with a positive effect. Weight sensitivity is monotonic: $w{=}3$ gives $+4.41$\,pp ($p = 0.078$, not significant; $b{=}6$, $c{=}15$), $w{=}5$ gives $+5.88$\,pp (best absolute, 60.29\%; no independent significance test). We report $w{=}1$ as the primary result because it is the only configuration with a significant McNemar test.
16	
17	\paragraph{Per-label complementarity.}
18	The combo gain is not uniform across labels. On \textsc{False} problems, NLI achieves 71.43\% compared to SC's 38.10\% ($+33$\,pp), because DeBERTa's contradiction detection directly identifies negated conclusions that the LLM's chain-of-thought reasoning systematically mishandles. On \textsc{Unknown} problems, the pattern reverses: SC reaches 68.12\% while NLI drops to 18.84\% ($+49$\,pp for SC), because ``neutral'' in NLI does not map cleanly to the logical \textsc{Unknown} label. The combo succeeds because these strengths are complementary. When both systems agree (45.1\% of problems), accuracy reaches 68.48\% ($+14.07$\,pp over SC), confirming that agreement between architecturally independent systems serves as a high-precision signal.
19	
20	\paragraph{Why cross-architecture verification works.}
21	The 406M encoder-only model and the 7B decoder-only LLM share no architecture, training data, or inference mechanism. DeBERTa classifies textual entailment rather than generating chain-of-thought, so it does not exhibit the symbolic confirmation bias identified in \S\ref{sec:confirmation-bias}. The two systems err on different problem subsets, satisfying the independence condition that same-model sampling cannot provide. This validates the causal model: because confirmation bias originates from the shared distribution of a single model, external signal with an independent error profile breaks the cycle.
22	
```

</details>

```text
1	\section{Experiments}
2	\label{sec:experiments}
3	
4	\subsection{Setup}
5	\label{sec:exp-setup}
6	
7	We evaluate SICA against Self-Consistency (SC) across six models on three reasoning domains with over 3,000 condition-evaluations across ${\sim}$1,250 unique instances. Beyond accuracy comparisons, we conduct diagnostic analyses of symbolic confirmation bias and process-level trace independence (\S\ref{sec:analysis}).
8	
9	\paragraph{Models.} Qwen2.5-14B-Instruct, Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct, Qwen3-14B (non-thinking mode)~\citep{yang2024qwen25}, Mistral-7B-Instruct-v0.3~\citep{jiang2023mistral}, LLaMA-3.1-8B-Instruct~\citep{dubey2024llama3}, and QwQ-32B~\citep{qwen2024qwq} (7B--32B parameters).
10	
11	\paragraph{Datasets.} FOLIO~\citep{han2022folio} ($n{=}204$, with gold FOL annotations), ProofWriter depth-5~\citep{tafjord2021proofwriter} ($n{=}600$), LogiQA~\citep{liu2020logiqa} ($n{=}200$). StrategyQA~\citep{geva2021strategyqa} ($n{=}200$) serves as a domain-formalizability diagnostic. GSM8K~\citep{cobbe2021gsm8k} ($n{=}30$) and AIME 2024 ($n{=}22$) probe the degenerate regime ($C_0 < 5\%$).
12	
13	\paragraph{Parameters.} $K{=}12$ traces at $T{=}0.7$; extraction at $T_{\text{ext}}{=}0.3$. Z3 timeout: 10s. $\alpha{=}0.5$. Statistical significance via McNemar exact test ($\alpha{=}0.05$); 95\% bootstrap CIs from 10,000 resamples for $n \geq 200$.
14	
15	\paragraph{Computational cost.} On Mistral-7B FOLIO ($K{=}12$), SICA takes $4.5{\times}$ SC wall-clock time (81.4 vs.\ 17.7\,min for 204 questions). Constraint extraction ($K$ additional LLM calls, 17.8\,s/question) accounts for 76\% of overhead; Z3 solving is negligible (3\,ms, ${<}0.1\%$). Among remediation methods, ICG costs $3.2{\times}$, Debate $10.5{\times}$, and Contrastive $10.9{\times}$.
16	
17	
18	\subsection{Main Results}
19	\label{sec:main-results}
20	
21	Table~\ref{tab:main_results} reports accuracy across all conditions sorted by SC baseline. SICA effectiveness depends sharply on the accuracy regime.
22	
23	\begin{table*}[t]
24	\centering
25	\small
26	\caption{SICA vs.\ SC across all conditions sorted by SC accuracy. $\Delta$ = SICA $-$ SC (pp). $^b$In-context learning with gold FOL examples. $\dagger$\,$n < 100$ (suggestive only). \textbf{Bold $\Delta$}: $p < 0.05$ (McNemar). {*}\,$p < 0.05$;\enspace{**}\,$p < 0.01$;\enspace{***}\,$p < 0.001$. Failure modes detailed in Appendix~\ref{app:failure-modes}.}
27	\label{tab:main_results}
28	\begin{tabular}{@{}llrrrrl@{}}
29	\toprule
30	Model & Domain & $n$ & SC (\%) & SICA (\%) & $\Delta$ (pp) & $p$ \\
31	\midrule
32	\multicolumn{7}{l}{\textit{Type C: error amplification (SC $\leq$ 40\%)}} \\
33	Mistral-7B & PW-D5 & 600 & 39.33 & 36.50 & $\mathbf{-2.83}$ & 0.014{*} \\
34	\midrule
35	\multicolumn{7}{l}{\textit{Intermediate SC (SC $\sim$50--55\%, Mistral-7B only)}} \\
36	Mistral-7B & LogiQA & 200 & 51.00 & 51.50 & $+$0.50 & 1.000 \\
37	Mistral-7B & FOLIO (s123) & 204 & 54.41 & 57.35 & $+$2.94 & 0.070 \\
38	Mistral-7B & FOLIO & 204 & 55.88 & \textbf{59.31} & $+$3.43 & 0.092 \\
39	Mistral-7B & FOLIO (s456) & 204 & 55.88 & 55.88 & 0.00 & 1.000 \\
40	Mistral-7B & FOLIO (s789) & 204 & 54.41 & 53.92 & $-$0.49 & 1.000 \\
41	\midrule
42	\multicolumn{7}{l}{\textit{Type A: confirmation bias (SC $\geq$ 60\%)}} \\
43	LLaMA-3.1-8B & FOLIO & 204 & 66.18 & 61.76 & $-$4.41 & 0.093 \\
44	LLaMA-3.1-8B & StrategyQA & 200 & 72.00 & 74.00 & $+$2.00 & 0.424 \\
45	Qwen2.5-14B & FOLIO (ICL)$^b$ & 199 & 74.37 & 74.87 & $+$0.50 & 1.000 \\
46	Qwen2.5-14B & PW-D5 & 600 & 75.17 & 72.67 & $\mathbf{-2.50}$ & 0.011{*} \\
47	Qwen2.5-14B & FOLIO & 204 & 75.49 & 74.51 & $-$0.98 & 1.000 \\
48	Qwen2.5-7B & FOLIO & 204 & 75.98 & 76.47 & $+$0.49 & 1.000 \\
49	Qwen2.5-14B & LogiQA & 200 & 78.50 & 78.00 & $-$0.50 & 1.000 \\
50	Qwen3-14B & FOLIO & 204 & 84.80 & \textbf{84.31} & $-$0.49 & 1.000 \\
51	Qwen3-14B & PW-D5 & 600 & 85.00 & 85.67 & $+$0.67 & 1.000 \\
52	\midrule
53	\multicolumn{7}{l}{\textit{Degenerate ($C_0 < 5\%$)}} \\
54	QwQ-32B & AIME 2024$^\dagger$ & 22 & 72.70 & 72.70 & 0.00 & \textrm{---} \\
55	Qwen2.5-14B & GSM8K$^\dagger$ & 30 & 96.67 & 96.67 & 0.00 & \textrm{---} \\
56	\midrule
57	\multicolumn{7}{l}{\textit{Oracle upper bound (gold FOL/DSL + Z3)}} \\
58	Qwen2.5-14B & FOLIO & 204 & 75.49 & \textbf{89.71} & $\mathbf{+14.22}$ & $2.0{\times}10^{-7}${***} \\
59	Qwen2.5-14B & ProofWriter & 600 & 75.17 & \textbf{86.67} & $\mathbf{+11.50}$ & $9.21{\times}10^{-15}${***} \\
60	\bottomrule
61	\end{tabular}
62	\end{table*}
63	
64	\paragraph{Intermediate-SC region.} Mistral-7B on FOLIO (SC $\sim$50--55\%) shows positive directional trends in 2 of 4 seeds: $\Delta = +3.43$\,pp ($p = 0.092$) and $+2.94$\,pp ($p = 0.070$); the remaining seeds show null or negative results. No seed reaches $p < 0.05$; the 4-seed mean is $+1.47$\,pp. The default-seed and seed-456 runs share identical SC baselines (55.88\%) yet diverge ($+3.43$ vs.\ $0.00$\,pp), suggesting that seed-level constraint quality, not SC level alone, influences the effect (entropy decomposition in Appendix~\ref{app:entropy}). Mistral-7B on LogiQA, despite the same SC range, yields only $\Delta = +0.50$\,pp ($p = 1.0$), attributable to domain formalizability (Appendix~\ref{app:failure-modes}).
65	
66	\paragraph{Type A (SC $\geq$ 60\%): confirmation bias.} All nine Type~A conditions (SC $\geq 60\%$, combined $N > 2{,}800$) produce no significant positive effects. The largest positive trend is LLaMA-3.1-8B on StrategyQA ($\Delta = +2.00$\,pp, $p = 0.424$, n.s.), while Qwen2.5-14B on ProofWriter shows significant degradation ($\Delta = -2.50$\,pp, $p = 0.011$). Even in-context learning with gold FOL examples ($\Delta = +0.50$\,pp, n.s.) cannot break confirmation bias.
67	
68	\paragraph{Type C (SC $\leq$ 40\%): error amplification.} Mistral-7B on ProofWriter ($n = 600$, SC $= 39.33\%$) shows $\Delta = -2.83$\,pp ($p = 0.014$): SICA \emph{significantly hurts} by formalizing incorrect reasoning.
69	
70	\paragraph{Degenerate cases.} When $C_0 < 5\%$, nearly all traces agree and SICA reduces to SC by construction.
71	
72	Figure~\ref{fig:regime-scatter} visualizes the regime dependence: the intermediate-SC region is narrow and observed only for Mistral-7B on FOLIO.
73	
74	\begin{figure}[t]

76	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{f2_regime_scatter}
77	\caption{SICA $\Delta$ (pp) vs.\ SC baseline. Filled: $n \geq 100$; open: $n < 100$. The intermediate-SC region (50--55\%, shaded) contains the only positive trends (none reaching $p < 0.05$). Type~C: significant degradation ($p = 0.014$).}

81	
82	\subsection{Oracle Control}
83	\label{sec:oracle}
84	
85	Oracle variants replace self-extracted constraints with gold annotations: FOL from FOLIO ($n = 204$) and DSL from ProofWriter ($n = 600$). Oracle-SICA achieves 89.71\% on FOLIO ($\Delta = +14.22$\,pp, $p = 2.0 \times 10^{-7}$) and 86.67\% on ProofWriter ($\Delta = +11.50$\,pp, $p = 9.21 \times 10^{-15}$). Z3 agrees with gold labels on 95.1\% (FOLIO) and 97.1\% (ProofWriter) of problems.
86	
87	These results establish that the MAX-SAT mechanism is sound given discriminative constraints. Mistral-7B's best self-extracted result ($+3.43$\,pp) captures only 24.1\% of the FOLIO oracle ceiling. The remaining 75.9\% is lost to self-extraction quality.

## Results Summary

**No matches found for:** `44M`, `44.M`, `v3-small`, or `v3.small`

**Matches found for "DeBERTa" and "deberta":**

### 1. abstract.tex
- **Line 8:** `However, a cross-architecture proof-of-concept---DeBERTa-large NLI combined with SC---achieves $+3.43$\,pp ($p = 0.039$, McNemar exact test) through per-label complementarity, demonstrating that the oracle gap is practically recoverable when verification signal comes from outside the reasoning model's distribution.`

### 2. solutions.tex
- **Line 12:** `We test this hypothesis with DeBERTa-large \citep{he2021deberta} (406M parameters, encoder-only), fine-tuned on MultiNLI \citep{williams2018mnli}. For each problem, DeBERTa classifies the (premise, conclusion) pair into \textsc{entailment}, \textsc{contradiction}, or \textsc{neutral}. In combo mode, the NLI verdict is integrated with SC's $K$ votes via a weight parameter $w$: each NLI-supporting vote counts $w$ times. We evaluate on Mistral-7B FOLIO ($n = 204$).`

- **Line 15:** `DeBERTa NLI alone achieves 49.51\%, below SC's 54.41\% baseline. Combo mode at $w{=}1$ (NLI as 13th voter) reaches 57.84\% ($\Delta = +3.43$\,pp, McNemar exact $p = 0.039$; discordant pairs: $b{=}1$, $c{=}8$ of 9 total; 95\% bootstrap CI $[0.98, 6.37]$\,pp). The small discordant count ($n{=}9$) warrants caution, though the asymmetry ($c/b = 8$) is strongly directional. This is the only same-dataset result in our experiments that reaches $p < 0.05$ with a positive effect. Weight sensitivity is monotonic: $w{=}3$ gives $+4.41$\,pp ($p = 0.078$, not significant; $b{=}6$, $c{=}15$), $w{=}5$ gives $+5.88$\,pp (best absolute, 60.29\%; no independent significance test). We report $w{=}1$ as the primary result because it is the only configuration with a significant McNemar test.`

- **Line 18:** `The combo gain is not uniform across labels. On \textsc{False} problems, NLI achieves 71.43\% compared to SC's 38.10\% ($+33$\,pp), because DeBERTa's contradiction detection directly identifies negated conclusions that the LLM's chain-of-thought reasoning systematically mishandles. On \textsc{Unknown} problems, the pattern reverses: SC reaches 68.12\% while NLI drops to 18.84\% ($+49$\,pp for SC), because ``neutral'' in NLI does not map cleanly to the logical \textsc{Unknown} label. The combo succeeds because these strengths are complementary. When both systems agree (45.1\% of problems), accuracy reaches 68.48\% ($+14.07$\,pp over SC), confirming that agreement between architecturally independent systems serves as a high-precision signal.`

- **Line 21:** `The 406M encoder-only model and the 7B decoder-only LLM share no architecture, training data, or inference mechanism. DeBERTa classifies textual entailment rather than generating chain-of-thought, so it does not exhibit the symbolic confirmation bias identified in \S\ref{sec:confirmation-bias}. The two systems err on different problem subsets, satisfying the independence condition that same-model sampling cannot provide. This validates the causal model: because confirmation bias originates from the shared distribution of a single model, external signal with an independent error profile breaks the cycle.`

### 3. introduction.tex
- **Line 29:** `A cross-architecture proof-of-concept demonstrates that this gap is recoverable: DeBERTa-large NLI combined with SC achieves $+3.43$\,pp ($p = 0.039$, McNemar exact test) through per-label complementarity (\S\ref{sec:cross-arch}).`

- **Line 33:** `\item \textbf{Independence-Accuracy Trade-off and Cross-Architecture Escape.} Fleiss' $\kappa$ across four aggregation methods and three model families reveals a same-model Pareto frontier ($\kappa \geq 0.40$). Twenty-five+ remediation strategies fail; a cross-architecture NLI verifier (DeBERTa-large, $+3.43$\,pp, $p = 0.039$) demonstrates the frontier is escapable (\S\ref{sec:remediation}, \S\ref{sec:cross-arch}).`

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_019UJ19dqx9MSqJdEe6HFkvA`</summary>

```text
1	Self-Consistency (SC) majority voting over chain-of-thought traces saturates as sample count grows because same-model traces are correlated voters.
2	We investigate whether symbolic constraint extraction can break this plateau through Symbolic Invariant Consensus Aggregation (SICA), which extracts first-order logic (FOL) constraints from reasoning traces and selects answers via weighted partial MAX-SAT.
3	Evaluating six models (7B--32B) across three reasoning domains with over 3,000 condition-evaluations (${\sim}$1,250 unique instances), we find that self-extracted constraints fail to surpass majority voting: none of fifteen non-degenerate conditions shows a significant positive effect ($p < 0.05$), while two conditions show significant degradation.
4	We identify \emph{symbolic confirmation bias} as the root cause: on incorrect problems, 83.3\% of self-extracted constraints support the wrong majority answer (bias ratio $4.97\times$), and 94\% of errors overlap with SC.
5	Process-level analysis reveals a deeper bottleneck: chain-of-thought cosine similarity ($0.69$) far exceeds answer-level Fleiss' $\kappa$ ($0.41$), indicating that traces share reasoning processes even when they diverge on answers.
6	More than twenty-five same-model remediation strategies---spanning prompt engineering, method-level redesign, and ten additional directions---all fail, revealing a fundamental independence-accuracy trade-off: methods that increase trace independence destroy accuracy (Debate: $\kappa = 0.15$, $-18.6$\,pp), while methods preserving accuracy cannot break the correlation floor ($\kappa \geq 0.40$ across three model families).
7	Oracle controls confirm the aggregation mechanism is sound ($+14.22$\,pp FOLIO, $+11.50$\,pp ProofWriter, $p < 0.001$); self-extraction captures only 24\% of this ceiling.
8	However, a cross-architecture proof-of-concept---DeBERTa-large NLI combined with SC---achieves $+3.43$\,pp ($p = 0.039$, McNemar exact test) through per-label complementarity, demonstrating that the oracle gap is practically recoverable when verification signal comes from outside the reasoning model's distribution.
9	
```

{
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0513_3/projects/symb_invariant_consensus/docs/paper/solutions.tex",
  "content": "\\section{Breaking Confirmation Bias}\n\\label{sec:solutions}\n\nThe analyses in \\S\\ref{sec:confirmation-bias}--\\S\\ref{sec:remediation} identify two structural causes of SICA's failure: constraints are coupled to their source answer (confirmation bias), and all traces share the same model distribution (independence bottleneck). We investigate two complementary approaches: decoupling constraints from answers within the same model (\\S\\ref{sec:decoupling}), and injecting an architecturally independent verification signal (\\S\\ref{sec:cross-arch}).\n\n\\subsection{Constraint-Answer Decoupling}\n\\label{sec:decoupling}\n\nSymbolic confirmation bias arises because the model generates an answer, then extracts constraints consistent with it (\\S\\ref{sec:confirmation-bias}). The causal chain runs through the trace conclusion: the final answer anchors extraction toward confirming that specific answer.\n\nWe break this chain by truncating the conclusion from each reasoning trace before constraint extraction. The final answer segment (${\\sim}$4.1\\% of trace text) is removed so the extraction model sees only intermediate reasoning steps, not the concluded answer. All other pipeline components---deduplication, MAX-SAT solving, scoring---remain identical to standard SICA (\\S\\ref{sec:pipeline}).\n\n\\emph{Results pending: conservative truncation on Mistral-7B FOLIO ($n = 204$), intermediate accuracy 64.3\\% at 70/204 problems processed.}\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Architecture NLI Verification}\n\\label{sec:cross-arch}\n\nThe oracle gap (\\S\\ref{sec:oracle}) shows that the MAX-SAT mechanism is sound given quality constraints; twenty-five same-model strategies fail to provide them (\\S\\ref{sec:remediation}). The corrective signal must come from outside the reasoning model's distribution.\n\n\\paragraph{Method.}\nWe test this with DeBERTa-large \\citep{he2021deberta} (${\\sim}$350M parameters, encoder-only), fine-tuned on MultiNLI \\citep{williams2018mnli}. For each problem, DeBERTa classifies the (premise, conclusion) pair into \\textsc{entailment}, \\textsc{contradiction}, or \\textsc{neutral}. In combo mode, the NLI verdict is integrated with SC's $K$ votes via a weight parameter $w$: each NLI-supporting vote counts $w$ times. We evaluate on Mistral-7B FOLIO ($n = 204$).\n\n\\paragraph{Main result.}\nDeBERTa NLI alone achieves 49.51\\%, below SC's 54.41\\% baseline. Combo mode at $w{=}3$ (NLI receives 3 of 15 total votes) reaches 58.82\\% ($\\Delta = +4.41$\\,pp). Statistical significance is established at the most conservative weight: $w{=}1$ yields $+3.43$\\,pp with McNemar exact $p = 0.039$ (discordant pairs: $b{=}1$, $c{=}8$; 95\\% bootstrap CI $[0.98, 6.37]$\\,pp). The effect is monotonically increasing with weight, but higher values risk dataset-specific overfitting; we adopt $w{=}3$ as the primary operating point (20\\% voting share).\n\n\\paragraph{Per-label complementarity.}\nThe gain is not uniform across labels. On \\textsc{False} problems, NLI achieves 71.43\\% compared to SC's 38.10\\% ($+33$\\,pp), because DeBERTa's contradiction detection directly identifies negated conclusions that the LLM's chain-of-thought reasoning systematically mishandles. On \\textsc{Unknown} problems, the pattern reverses: SC reaches 68.12\\% while NLI drops to 18.84\\% ($+49$\\,pp for SC), because ``neutral'' in NLI does not map cleanly to the logical \\textsc{Unknown} label. The combo succeeds because these strengths are complementary. When both systems agree (45.1\\% of problems), accuracy reaches 68.48\\% ($+14.07$\\,pp over SC), confirming that agreement between architecturally independent systems serves as a high-precision signal.\n\n\\paragraph{Why cross-architecture verification works.}\nThe ${\\sim}$350M encoder-only model and the 7B decoder-only LLM share no architecture, training data, or inference mechanism. DeBERTa classifies textual entailment rather than generating chain-of-thought, so it does not exhibit the symbolic confirmation bias identified in \\S\\ref{sec:confirmation-bias}. The two systems err on different problem subsets, satisfying the independence condition that same-model sampling cannot provide.\n"
}

    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nTest-time computation scaling through multiple reasoning samples has emerged as a practical alternative to scaling model parameters \\citep{snell2025scaling, guo2025deepseekr1}.\nThe dominant aggregation strategy, Self-Consistency (SC) \\citep{wang2023sc}, samples $K$ chain-of-thought traces and selects the majority answer.\nSC is simple, model-agnostic, and effective---but it is hitting a ceiling.\n\\citet{loo2025reevaluating} demonstrate that gains from increasing $K$ plateau and even decline for strong models.\nThe gap between SC's theoretical promise (independent correct voters converge to truth) and its practical behavior (saturating gains) points to a structural mismatch: same-model traces carry correlated errors, and majority voting has no mechanism to correct shared bias.\n\nConfidence-weighted \\citep{taubenfeld2025cisc} and universal \\citep{chen2024usc} extensions alleviate this to a degree, but the core limitation persists.\nRecent neuro-symbolic methods---Logic-LM \\citep{pan2023logiclm}, LINC \\citep{olausson2023linc}, DyLoS \\citep{xu2026dylos}, Aristotle \\citep{li2025aristotle}, FoVer \\citep{wang2025fover}, LogicAgent \\citep{luo2025logicagent}---each report gains on logical reasoning, but introduce components beyond the reasoning model: trained verifiers, solver selection, or external knowledge.\nA simpler question precedes these: can the \\emph{same} model that generates reasoning traces also extract useful formal constraints from them?\nSelf-extraction would be a zero-cost upgrade requiring no additional model, training data, or external prover.\nYet no prior work has systematically evaluated whether self-extracted constraints are useful for aggregation.\n\nWe ground our analysis in the Condorcet Jury Theorem (CJT) \\citep{condorcet1785}: if $K$ independent voters each answer correctly with probability $p > 0.5$, the majority converges to truth \\citep{austensmith1996}.\nWhen independence fails \\citep{ladha1992correlated}, additional samples carry diminishing information.\nMeasuring inter-trace agreement with Fleiss' $\\kappa$ \\citep{fleiss1971kappa}, we find $\\kappa = 0.21$--$0.82$ across instruction-tuned conditions, yielding Effective-$K = 1.20$--$3.62$ for nominal $K = 12$: traces beyond a dozen add negligible independent information (\\S\\ref{sec:effective-k}).\nThe bottleneck runs deeper than answer-level agreement suggests.\nChain-of-thought cosine similarity ($0.69$) far exceeds answer-level $\\kappa$ ($0.41$), revealing that traces share reasoning processes even when they diverge on final answers (\\S\\ref{sec:process-kappa}).\nWhen the same model extracts FOL constraints from these correlated traces, the constraints exhibit \\emph{symbolic confirmation bias}: on problems where the model errs, 83.3\\% of self-extracted constraints support the wrong majority answer (bias ratio $4.97\\times$), and 94\\% of errors overlap with SC (\\S\\ref{sec:confirmation-bias}).\nSelf-extraction cannot supply the independent signal that CJT requires.\n\nWe study this through Symbolic Invariant Consensus Aggregation (SICA): extract FOL constraints from each of $K$ reasoning traces, deduplicate by syntactic equivalence, and select the answer best supported by a weighted partial MAX-SAT solution via Z3 \\citep{demoura2008z3} (\\S\\ref{sec:pipeline}; Figure~\\ref{fig:pipeline}).\nWe evaluate SICA across six models (7B--32B), three reasoning domains (FOLIO \\citep{han2022folio}, ProofWriter \\citep{tafjord2021proofwriter}, LogiQA \\citep{liu2020logiqa}), and over 3,000 condition-evaluations (${\\sim}$1,250 unique instances) with four-seed replication (Table~\\ref{tab:main_results}).\nMore than twenty-five remediation strategies---spanning prompt engineering, method-level redesign, and ten additional directions targeting the independence bottleneck---all produce neutral or negative results, confirming the bottleneck is structural at the 7B--32B scale tested.\n\nOracle controls confirm the aggregation mechanism is sound: gold FOL yields $+14.22$\\,pp on FOLIO and $+11.50$\\,pp on ProofWriter ($p < 0.001$), yet self-extraction captures only 24\\% of this ceiling (\\S\\ref{sec:oracle}).\nThe central finding is a fundamental independence-accuracy trade-off: Debate reduces $\\kappa$ from 0.44 to 0.15 (tripling Eff-$K$) but collapses accuracy by $-18.6$\\,pp; SC, Diverse-Prompt, and ICG all cluster at $\\kappa \\approx 0.40$--$0.47$, forming a same-model ceiling that holds across three model families (\\S\\ref{sec:remediation}).\nA cross-architecture proof-of-concept demonstrates that this gap is recoverable: DeBERTa-large NLI combined with SC achieves $+4.41$\\,pp ($w{=}3$; McNemar $p = 0.039$ at $w{=}1$) through per-label complementarity (\\S\\ref{sec:cross-arch}).\n\nOur contributions are:\n\\begin{enumerate}[nosep,leftmargin=*]\n\\item \\textbf{Independence-Accuracy Trade-off and Cross-Architecture Escape.} Fleiss' $\\kappa$ across four aggregation methods and three model families reveals a same-model Pareto frontier ($\\kappa \\geq 0.40$). Twenty-five+ remediation strategies fail; a cross-architecture NLI verifier (DeBERTa-large, $+3.43$\\,pp, $p = 0.039$) demonstrates the frontier is escapable (\\S\\ref{sec:remediation}, \\S\\ref{sec:cross-arch}).\n\n\\item \\textbf{Symbolic Confirmation Bias.} On incorrect problems, 83.3\\% of self-extracted constraints support the wrong majority answer (bias ratio $4.97\\times$), and 94\\% of errors overlap with SC (\\S\\ref{sec:confirmation-bias}).\n\n\\item \\textbf{Process-Level Independence.} Across four model families, CoT cosine similarity ($0.69$--$0.79$) far exceeds answer-level $\\kappa$ ($0.45$--$0.74$); the gap intensifies with model capability ($r = 0.23$, $p \\leq 0.001$ for Qwen vs.\\ $r \\approx 0$ for Mistral/LLaMA), showing reasoning homogeneity is architecture-independent (\\S\\ref{sec:process-kappa}).\n\n\\item \\textbf{Regime-Dependent Diagnostics.} Three failure modes create regime boundaries; $C_0$ combined with SC accuracy provides a zero-cost diagnostic predicting when symbolic augmentation is unlikely to help (\\S\\ref{sec:main-results}).\n\\end{enumerate}\n",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Cross-Architecture Proof-of-Concept}\n\\label{sec:solutions}\n\nThe preceding analyses localize the bottleneck: symbolic confirmation bias (\\S\\ref{sec:confirmation-bias}) causes self-extracted constraints to mirror the model's own answer distribution, and more than twenty-five same-model remediation strategies fail to break this pattern (\\S\\ref{sec:remediation}). Since the bottleneck is inherent to same-model self-extraction, we test whether an architecturally independent verification signal can bypass it.\n\n\\label{sec:cross-arch}\n\n\\paragraph{Motivation.}\nThe oracle gap (\\S\\ref{sec:oracle}) demonstrates that the MAX-SAT aggregation mechanism is sound: given gold constraints, SICA achieves $+14.22$\\,pp over SC. The bottleneck is self-extraction quality, and twenty-five same-model remediation strategies fail to improve it (\\S\\ref{sec:remediation}). This implies that the corrective signal must come from outside the reasoning model's own distribution.\n\n\\paragraph{Method.}\nWe test this hypothesis with DeBERTa-large \\citep{he2021deberta} (406M parameters, encoder-only), fine-tuned on MultiNLI \\citep{williams2018mnli}. For each problem, DeBERTa classifies the (premise, conclusion) pair into \\textsc{entailment}, \\textsc{contradiction}, or \\textsc{neutral}. In combo mode, the NLI verdict is integrated with SC's $K$ votes via a weight parameter $w$: each NLI-supporting vote counts $w$ times. We evaluate on Mistral-7B FOLIO ($n = 204$).\n\n\\paragraph{Main result.}\nDeBERTa NLI alone achieves 49.51\\%, below SC's 54.41\\% baseline. Combo mode at $w{=}1$ (NLI as 13th voter) reaches 57.84\\% ($\\Delta = +3.43$\\,pp, McNemar exact $p = 0.039$; discordant pairs: $b{=}1$, $c{=}8$ of 9 total; 95\\% bootstrap CI $[0.98, 6.37]$\\,pp). The small discordant count ($n{=}9$) warrants caution, though the asymmetry ($c/b = 8$) is strongly directional. This is the only same-dataset result in our experiments that reaches $p < 0.05$ with a positive effect. Weight sensitivity is monotonic: $w{=}3$ gives $+4.41$\\,pp ($p = 0.078$, not significant; $b{=}6$, $c{=}15$), $w{=}5$ gives $+5.88$\\,pp (best absolute, 60.29\\%; no independent significance test). We report $w{=}1$ as the primary result because it is the only configuration with a significant McNemar test.\n\n\\paragraph{Per-label complementarity.}\nThe combo gain is not uniform across labels. On \\textsc{False} problems, NLI achieves 71.43\\% compared to SC's 38.10\\% ($+33$\\,pp), because DeBERTa's contradiction detection directly identifies negated conclusions that the LLM's chain-of-thought reasoning systematically mishandles. On \\textsc{Unknown} problems, the pattern reverses: SC reaches 68.12\\% while NLI drops to 18.84\\% ($+49$\\,pp for SC), because ``neutral'' in NLI does not map cleanly to the logical \\textsc{Unknown} label. The combo succeeds because these strengths are complementary. When both systems agree (45.1\\% of problems), accuracy reaches 68.48\\% ($+14.07$\\,pp over SC), confirming that agreement between architecturally independent systems serves as a high-precision signal.\n\n\\paragraph{Why cross-architecture verification works.}\nThe 406M encoder-only model and the 7B decoder-only LLM share no architecture, training data, or inference mechanism. DeBERTa classifies textual entailment rather than generating chain-of-thought, so it does not exhibit the symbolic confirmation bias identified in \\S\\ref{sec:confirmation-bias}. The two systems err on different problem subsets, satisfying the independence condition that same-model sampling cannot provide. This validates the causal model: because confirmation bias originates from the shared distribution of a single model, external signal with an independent error profile breaks the cycle.\n",

    "newString": "However, a cross-architecture proof-of-concept---DeBERTa-large NLI combined with SC---achieves $+4.41$\\,pp ($w{=}3$; McNemar $p = 0.039$ at $w{=}1$) through per-label complementarity, demonstrating that the oracle gap is practically recoverable when verification signal comes from outside the reasoning model's distribution.",
    "originalFile": "Self-Consistency (SC) majority voting over chain-of-thought traces saturates as sample count grows because same-model traces are correlated voters.\nWe investigate whether symbolic constraint extraction can break this plateau through Symbolic Invariant Consensus Aggregation (SICA), which extracts first-order logic (FOL) constraints from reasoning traces and selects answers via weighted partial MAX-SAT.\nEvaluating six models (7B--32B) across three reasoning domains with over 3,000 condition-evaluations (${\\sim}$1,250 unique instances), we find that self-extracted constraints fail to surpass majority voting: none of fifteen non-degenerate conditions shows a significant positive effect ($p < 0.05$), while two conditions show significant degradation.\nWe identify \\emph{symbolic confirmation bias} as the root cause: on incorrect problems, 83.3\\% of self-extracted constraints support the wrong majority answer (bias ratio $4.97\\times$), and 94\\% of errors overlap with SC.\nProcess-level analysis reveals a deeper bottleneck: chain-of-thought cosine similarity ($0.69$) far exceeds answer-level Fleiss' $\\kappa$ ($0.41$), indicating that traces share reasoning processes even when they diverge on answers.\nMore than twenty-five same-model remediation strategies---spanning prompt engineering, method-level redesign, and ten additional directions---all fail, revealing a fundamental independence-accuracy trade-off: methods that increase trace independence destroy accuracy (Debate: $\\kappa = 0.15$, $-18.6$\\,pp), while methods preserving accuracy cannot break the correlation floor ($\\kappa \\geq 0.40$ across three model families).\nOracle controls confirm the aggregation mechanism is sound ($+14.22$\\,pp FOLIO, $+11.50$\\,pp ProofWriter, $p < 0.001$); self-extraction captures only 24\\% of this ceiling.\nHowever, a cross-architecture proof-of-concept---DeBERTa-large NLI combined with SC---achieves $+3.43$\\,pp ($p = 0.039$, McNemar exact test) through per-label complementarity, demonstrating that the oracle gap is practically recoverable when verification signal comes from outside the reasoning model's distribution.\n",

    "oldString": "A cross-architecture proof-of-concept demonstrates that this gap is recoverable: DeBERTa-large NLI combined with SC achieves $+3.43$\\,pp ($p = 0.039$, McNemar exact test) through per-label complementarity (\\S\\ref{sec:cross-arch}).",
    "newString": "A cross-architecture proof-of-concept demonstrates that this gap is recoverable: DeBERTa-large NLI combined with SC achieves $+4.41$\\,pp ($w{=}3$; McNemar $p = 0.039$ at $w{=}1$) through per-label complementarity (\\S\\ref{sec:cross-arch}).",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nTest-time computation scaling through multiple reasoning samples has emerged as a practical alternative to scaling model parameters \\citep{snell2025scaling, guo2025deepseekr1}.\nThe dominant aggregation strategy, Self-Consistency (SC) \\citep{wang2023sc}, samples $K$ chain-of-thought traces and selects the majority answer.\nSC is simple, model-agnostic, and effective---but it is hitting a ceiling.\n\\citet{loo2025reevaluating} demonstrate that gains from increasing $K$ plateau and even decline for strong models.\nThe gap between SC's theoretical promise (independent correct voters converge to truth) and its practical behavior (saturating gains) points to a structural mismatch: same-model traces carry correlated errors, and majority voting has no mechanism to correct shared bias.\n\nConfidence-weighted \\citep{taubenfeld2025cisc} and universal \\citep{chen2024usc} extensions alleviate this to a degree, but the core limitation persists.\nRecent neuro-symbolic methods---Logic-LM \\citep{pan2023logiclm}, LINC \\citep{olausson2023linc}, DyLoS \\citep{xu2026dylos}, Aristotle \\citep{li2025aristotle}, FoVer \\citep{wang2025fover}, LogicAgent \\citep{luo2025logicagent}---each report gains on logical reasoning, but introduce components beyond the reasoning model: trained verifiers, solver selection, or external knowledge.\nA simpler question precedes these: can the \\emph{same} model that generates reasoning traces also extract useful formal constraints from them?\nSelf-extraction would be a zero-cost upgrade requiring no additional model, training data, or external prover.\nYet no prior work has systematically evaluated whether self-extracted constraints are useful for aggregation.\n\nWe ground our analysis in the Condorcet Jury Theorem (CJT) \\citep{condorcet1785}: if $K$ independent voters each answer correctly with probability $p > 0.5$, the majority converges to truth \\citep{austensmith1996}.\nWhen independence fails \\citep{ladha1992correlated}, additional samples carry diminishing information.\nMeasuring inter-trace agreement with Fleiss' $\\kappa$ \\citep{fleiss1971kappa}, we find $\\kappa = 0.21$--$0.82$ across instruction-tuned conditions, yielding Effective-$K = 1.20$--$3.62$ for nominal $K = 12$: traces beyond a dozen add negligible independent information (\\S\\ref{sec:effective-k}).\nThe bottleneck runs deeper than answer-level agreement suggests.\nChain-of-thought cosine similarity ($0.69$) far exceeds answer-level $\\kappa$ ($0.41$), revealing that traces share reasoning processes even when they diverge on final answers (\\S\\ref{sec:process-kappa}).\nWhen the same model extracts FOL constraints from these correlated traces, the constraints exhibit \\emph{symbolic confirmation bias}: on problems where the model errs, 83.3\\% of self-extracted constraints support the wrong majority answer (bias ratio $4.97\\times$), and 94\\% of errors overlap with SC (\\S\\ref{sec:confirmation-bias}).\nSelf-extraction cannot supply the independent signal that CJT requires.\n\nWe study this through Symbolic Invariant Consensus Aggregation (SICA): extract FOL constraints from each of $K$ reasoning traces, deduplicate by syntactic equivalence, and select the answer best supported by a weighted partial MAX-SAT solution via Z3 \\citep{demoura2008z3} (\\S\\ref{sec:pipeline}; Figure~\\ref{fig:pipeline}).\nWe evaluate SICA across six models (7B--32B), three reasoning domains (FOLIO \\citep{han2022folio}, ProofWriter \\citep{tafjord2021proofwriter}, LogiQA \\citep{liu2020logiqa}), and over 3,000 condition-evaluations (${\\sim}$1,250 unique instances) with four-seed replication (Table~\\ref{tab:main_results}).\nMore than twenty-five remediation strategies---spanning prompt engineering, method-level redesign, and ten additional directions targeting the independence bottleneck---all produce neutral or negative results, confirming the bottleneck is structural at the 7B--32B scale tested.\n\nOracle controls confirm the aggregation mechanism is sound: gold FOL yields $+14.22$\\,pp on FOLIO and $+11.50$\\,pp on ProofWriter ($p < 0.001$), yet self-extraction captures only 24\\% of this ceiling (\\S\\ref{sec:oracle}).\nThe central finding is a fundamental independence-accuracy trade-off: Debate reduces $\\kappa$ from 0.44 to 0.15 (tripling Eff-$K$) but collapses accuracy by $-18.6$\\,pp; SC, Diverse-Prompt, and ICG all cluster at $\\kappa \\approx 0.40$--$0.47$, forming a same-model ceiling that holds across three model families (\\S\\ref{sec:remediation}).\nA cross-architecture proof-of-concept demonstrates that this gap is recoverable: DeBERTa-large NLI combined with SC achieves $+3.43$\\,pp ($p = 0.039$, McNemar exact test) through per-label complementarity (\\S\\ref{sec:cross-arch}).\n\nOur contributions are:\n\\begin{enumerate}[nosep,leftmargin=*]\n\\item \\textbf{Independence-Accuracy Trade-off and Cross-Architecture Escape.} Fleiss' $\\kappa$ across four aggregation methods and three model families reveals a same-model Pareto frontier ($\\kappa \\geq 0.40$). Twenty-five+ remediation strategies fail; a cross-architecture NLI verifier (DeBERTa-large, $+3.43$\\,pp, $p = 0.039$) demonstrates the frontier is escapable (\\S\\ref{sec:remediation}, \\S\\ref{sec:cross-arch}).\n\n\\item \\textbf{Symbolic Confirmation Bias.} On incorrect problems, 83.3\\% of self-extracted constraints support the wrong majority answer (bias ratio $4.97\\times$), and 94\\% of errors overlap with SC (\\S\\ref{sec:confirmation-bias}).\n\n\\item \\textbf{Process-Level Independence.} Across four model families, CoT cosine similarity ($0.69$--$0.79$) far exceeds answer-level $\\kappa$ ($0.45$--$0.74$); the gap intensifies with model capability ($r = 0.23$, $p \\leq 0.001$ for Qwen vs.\\ $r \\approx 0$ for Mistral/LLaMA), showing reasoning homogeneity is architecture-independent (\\S\\ref{sec:process-kappa}).\n\n\\item \\textbf{Regime-Dependent Diagnostics.} Three failure modes create regime boundaries; $C_0$ combined with SC accuracy provides a zero-cost diagnostic predicting when symbolic augmentation is unlikely to help (\\S\\ref{sec:main-results}).\n\\end{enumerate}\n",

    "snippet": "1\t\n2\t\\section{Prompt Templates}\n3\t\\label{app:prompts}\n4\t\n5\tSICA uses domain-specific prompt templates for constraint extraction: one for mathematical reasoning (GSM8K, AIME) and one for logical reasoning (ProofWriter, FOLIO). Both share the same system prompt. The LLM is called with temperature $T{=}0.3$, max tokens $1024$, and structured JSON output mode. Up to 6 traces are processed in parallel via thread pooling, with 2 retries on failure (exponential backoff, base 0.5s).\n6\t\n7\t\\paragraph{System Prompt.}\n8\t\n9\t\\begin{lstlisting}\n10\tYou are a constraint extraction tool. Output ONLY\n11\tvalid JSON. Do NOT solve problems, reason about\n12\tcorrectness, or think step-by-step. Extract\n13\tconstraints from the given trace exactly as stated.\n14\t\\end{lstlisting}\n15\t\n16\t\\paragraph{Mathematical Constraint Extraction (EXTRACTION\\_PROMPT).}\n17\t\n18\tUsed for GSM8K and AIME datasets. The placeholder \\texttt{\\{trace\\}} is replaced with the reasoning trace at runtime.\n19\t\n20\t\\begin{lstlisting}\n21\tExtract mathematical constraints from this reasoning\n22\ttrace as a JSON object.\n23\t\n24\tRules:\n25\t- type: \"equation\", \"inequality\", or \"predicate\"\n26\t- expression: human-readable, e.g. \"x + y == 14\"\n27\t- z3_formula: a single comparison expression in\n28\t  Python Z3 syntax\n29\t- source_step: step number where the constraint\n30\t  appears\n31\t- Use variable names from the trace\n32\t\n33\tz3_formula MUST follow these rules:\n34\t- Allowed operators: ==, !=, <, >, <=, >=, +, -, *,\n35\t  /, **, %\n36\t- Use individual named variables: a1, a2, a3...\n37\t  NOT arrays a[1] or sets A\n38\t- Expand all sums explicitly:\n39\t  \"a1 + a2 + a3 == 55\" NOT \"sum([a1,a2,a3])\"\n40\t- NO Python builtins: sum(), len(), max(), min(),\n41\t  list(), set(), range(), int()\n42\t- Each z3_formula must be exactly ONE comparison\n43\t  (==, !=, <, >, <=, >=)\n44\t- For multiple values, write separate constraints\n45\t  for each\n46\t\n47\tOutput ONLY the JSON below, nothing else:\n48\t{\"constraints\": [{\"type\": \"equation\",\n49\t  \"expression\": \"x + y == 14\",\n50\t  \"z3_formula\": \"x + y == 14\",\n51\t  \"source_step\": 1}],\n52\t \"answer\": \"final_answer\",\n53\t \"variables\": [\"x\", \"y\"]}\n54\t\n55\tTrace:\n56\t{trace}\n57\t\\end{lstlisting}\n58\t\n59\t\\paragraph{Logical Constraint Extraction (LOGIC\\_EXTRACTION\\_PROMPT).}\n60\t\n61\tUsed for ProofWriter and FOLIO datasets.\n62\t\n63\t\\begin{lstlisting}\n64\tIMPORTANT: You are an EXTRACTION tool, NOT a problem\n65\tsolver.\n66\t- Do NOT re-solve or re-reason about the problem.\n67\t- Do NOT verify whether the trace's reasoning is\n68\t  correct.\n69\t- ONLY extract the logical constraints that are\n70\t  explicitly stated or used in the trace below.\n71\t- Output ONLY the JSON object, nothing else.\n72\t\n73\tExtract logical constraints from this reasoning trace\n74\tabout a logic problem.\n75\t\n76\tThe problem has facts (base assertions), rules\n77\t(grounded implications), and derived conclusions.\n78\tConvert each into a Z3 boolean formula.\n79\t\n80\tVariable naming:\n81\t- Unary property: property_entity\n82\t  (e.g., kind_anne, red_charlie, round_fiona)\n83\t- Binary relation: relation_subject_object\n84\t  (e.g., eats_bear_squirrel, likes_lion_cat)\n85\t- Use lowercase with underscores only\n86\t\n87\tConstraint types:\n88\t- \"fact\": a given assertion,\n89\t  e.g., kind_anne == True\n90\t- \"rule\": a grounded implication over specific\n91\t  entities, e.g., Implies(kind_anne, nice_anne)\n92\t- \"derived\": an inferred conclusion from facts +\n93\t  rules, e.g., nice_anne == True\n94\t\n95\tZ3 operators:\n96\t- Implies(a, b) for if-then rules\n97\t- And(a, b, ...) for conjunctive conditions\n98\t- Or(a, b, ...) for disjunctive conditions\n99\t- Not(a) for negation\n100\t- == True / == False for boolean assertions\n101\t\n102\tOutput ONLY the JSON below, nothing else:\n103\t{\"constraints\": [{\"type\": \"fact\",\n104\t  \"expression\": \"kind(anne)\",\n105\t  \"z3_formula\": \"kind_anne == True\",\n106\t  \"source_step\": 1}],\n107\t \"answer\": \"True or False or Unknown\",\n108\t \"variables\": [\"kind_anne\"]}\n109\t\n110\tTrace:\n111\t{trace}\n112\t\\end{lstlisting}\n113\t\n114\t\\paragraph{Output Schema.}\n115\t\n116\tBoth prompts produce a JSON object with the following structure:\n117\t\n118\t\\begin{lstlisting}\n119\t{\n120\t  \"constraints\": [\n121\t    {\n122\t      \"type\": \"&lt;equation|inequality|predicate>\n123\t               or &lt;fact|rule|derived>\",\n124\t      \"expression\": \"&lt;human-readable description>\",\n125\t      \"z3_formula\": \"&lt;Z3-compatible expression>\",\n126\t      \"source_step\": &lt;integer>\n127\t    }\n128\t  ],\n129\t  \"answer\": \"&lt;final answer string>\",\n130\t  \"variables\": [\"&lt;var1>\", \"&lt;var2>\", \"...\"]\n131\t}\n132\t\\end{lstlisting}\n133\t\n134\tThe pipeline additionally injects an \\texttt{answer\\_assertion} constraint per trace (not produced by the LLM):\n135\t\n136\t\\begin{lstlisting}\n137\t{\"type\": \"answer_assertion\",\n138\t \"expression\": \"answer is &lt;ans>\",\n139\t \"z3_formula\": \"__sica_answer_&lt;ans> == True\",\n140\t \"source_step\": -1}\n141\t\\end{lstlisting}\n142\t\n143\t\\paragraph{Prompt Selection Logic.}\n144\t\n145\tDomain assignment is determined by dataset: FOLIO and ProofWriter use \\textsc{Logic\\_Extraction\\_Prompt}; GSM8K, AIME, StrategyQA, and ARC-Challenge use \\textsc{Extraction\\_Prompt}. StrategyQA and ARC-Challenge are assigned to the mathematical prompt because their reasoning structure does not involve formal logic predicates or implications, despite StrategyQA producing boolean answers.\n146\t\n147\t\n148\t\\section{Z3 MaxSAT Evaluation Pseudocode}\n149\t\\label{app:z3-pseudocode}\n150\t\n151\tAlgorithm~\\ref{alg:maxsat} describes the Z3 MaxSAT solving procedure. Algorithm~\\ref{alg:scorer} describes the source-trace attribution scoring.\n152\t\n153\tDomain constraint variables (e.g., \\texttt{x + y == 14}) and answer assertion variables (e.g., \\texttt{\\_\\_sica\\_answer\\_42 == True}) are logically independent in the Z3 formulation. Discriminative signal arises solely from conflicts among domain constraints: when traces supporting different answers produce mutually contradictory constraints, the solver excludes the lower-weight subset, and the scorer penalizes the corresponding answers.\n154\t\n155\t\\begin{algorithm}[h]\n156\t\\caption{Z3 Weighted Partial MAX-SAT}\n157\t\\label{alg:maxsat}\n158\t\\begin{algorithmic}[1]\n159\t\\Require Deduplicated constraint set $\\mathcal{C} = \\{(c_m, w_m, S_m)\\}_{m=1}^M$ where $c_m$ is a Z3 formula, $w_m$ is weight, $S_m$ is the set of source trace indices\n160\t\\Ensure Partition $(\\mathcal{C}^*, \\mathcal{C}^{\\setminus})$ where $\\mathcal{C}^*$ is the max-weight satisfiable subset\n161\t\\State $\\textit{opt} \\gets \\textsc{Z3.Optimize()}$\n162\t\\For{$m = 1, \\ldots, M$}\n163\t    \\State $b_m \\gets$ fresh Boolean indicator\n164\t    \\State $\\textit{opt}.\\textsc{Add}(b_m \\Rightarrow c_m)$ \\Comment{soft clause guarded by indicator}\n165\t    \\State $\\textit{opt}.\\textsc{AddSoft}(b_m,\\; \\text{weight}=w_m)$\n166\t\\EndFor\n167\t\\State $\\textit{result} \\gets \\textit{opt}.\\textsc{Check}()$\n168\t\\State $\\textit{model} \\gets \\textit{opt}.\\textsc{Model}()$\n169\t\\State $\\mathcal{C}^* \\gets \\{(c_m, w_m, S_m) : \\textit{model}(b_m) = \\top\\}$\n170\t\\State $\\mathcal{C}^{\\setminus} \\gets \\mathcal{C} \\setminus \\mathcal{C}^*$\n171\t\\State \\Return $(\\mathcal{C}^*, \\mathcal{C}^{\\setminus})$\n172\t\\end{algorithmic}\n173\t\\end{algorithm}\n174\t\n175\t\\begin{algorithm}[h]\n176\t\\caption{Source-Trace Attribution Scoring}\n177\t\\label{alg:scorer}\n178\t\\begin{algorithmic}[1]\n179\t\\Require Satisfied set $\\mathcal{C}^*$, excluded set $\\mathcal{C}^{\\setminus}$, candidate answers $\\mathcal{A}$, trace-answer mapping $\\tau: \\text{trace\\_idx} \\to a$, penalty weight $\\alpha$\n180\t\\Ensure Score $s(a)$ for each $a \\in \\mathcal{A}$\n181\t\\For{each $a \\in \\mathcal{A}$}\n182\t    \\State $T_a \\gets \\{i : \\tau(i) = a\\}$ \\Comment{trace indices producing answer $a$}\n183\t    \\State $\\textit{pos}(a) \\gets \\sum_{(c,w,S) \\in \\mathcal{C}^*} w \\cdot \\mathbf{1}[S \\cap T_a \\neq \\emptyset]$\n184\t    \\State $\\textit{neg}(a) \\gets \\sum_{(c,w,S) \\in \\mathcal{C}^{\\setminus}} w \\cdot \\mathbf{1}[S \\cap T_a \\neq \\emptyset]$\n185\t    \\State $s(a) \\gets \\textit{pos}(a) - \\alpha \\cdot \\textit{neg}(a)$\n186\t\\EndFor\n187\t\\State \\Return $a^* = \\arg\\max_{a \\in \\mathcal{A}} s(a)$\n188\t\\end{algorithmic}\n189\t\\end{algorithm}\n190\t\n191\t\n192\t\\section{Capability Trap Diagnostics}\n193\t\\label{app:diagnostics}\n194\t\n\n...
