    "snippet": "1\t\\section{Analysis}\n2\t\\label{sec:analysis}\n3\t\n4\tThis section investigates \\emph{why} SICA fails: trace correlation (\\S\\ref{sec:effective-k}), symbolic confirmation bias (\\S\\ref{sec:confirmation-bias}), process-level independence (\\S\\ref{sec:process-kappa}), supporting diagnostics (\\S\\ref{sec:diagnostics}), and remediation strategies (\\S\\ref{sec:remediation}).\n5\t\n6\t\\subsection{Condorcet Effective-K}\n7\t\\label{sec:effective-k}\n8\t\n9\tTable~\\ref{tab:independence} reports Fleiss' $\\kappa$ and Effective-K (Eq.~\\ref{eq:effk}) across 15 conditions at $K = 12$. Among instruction-tuned models, $\\kappa$ ranges from 0.21 to 0.82, yielding $K_{\\text{eff}} = 1.20\\text{--}3.62$. The four Mistral-7B FOLIO seeds produce $\\kappa \\in [0.41, 0.51]$, yet $\\Delta$ ranges from $+3.43$ to $-0.49$\\,pp---seed-level constraint quality, not independence alone, appears to influence the variation. Two conditions are jointly necessary for positive $\\Delta$: sufficient $K_{\\text{eff}}$ ($\\leq 1.30$ never improves) and sufficient SC accuracy ($K_{\\text{eff}} = 3.62$ with SC $= 39\\%$ produces significant degradation). Cross-model validation confirms universality: all four families produce $\\kappa \\geq 0.41$ (Table~\\ref{tab:kappa_generalization}; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model-kappa}).\n10\t\n11\t\\begin{table}[t]\n12\t\\centering\n13\t\\small\n14\t\\caption{Trace independence metrics ($K = 12$, sorted by $\\kappa$ descending). $\\kappa$: Fleiss' Kappa. $K_{\\text{eff}}$: Effective-K (Eq.~\\ref{eq:effk}). SC: Self-Consistency baseline. $\\Delta$: SICA $-$ SC (pp). {*}\\,$p < 0.05$; positive $\\Delta$ values do not reach significance. multi-T $= T \\in \\{0.3, 0.7, 1.2\\}$, 4 traces each.}\n15\t\\label{tab:independence}\n16\t\\begin{tabular}{@{}llccrr@{}}\n17\t\\toprule\n18\tModel & Condition & $\\kappa$ & $K_{\\text{eff}}$ & SC (\\%) & $\\Delta$ (pp) \\\\\n19\t\\midrule\n20\tQwen3 (think) & FOLIO & 0.91 & 1.09 & ${\\sim}$84 & ${\\sim}$0 \\\\\n21\tQwen2.5-14B & LogiQA & 0.82 & 1.20 & 78.50 & $-$0.50 \\\\\n22\tQwen3 & FOLIO & 0.77 & 1.26 & 84.80 & $-$0.49 \\\\\n23\tQwen2.5-14B & FOLIO & 0.74 & 1.30 & 75.49 & $-$0.98 \\\\\n24\tQwen2.5-7B & FOLIO & 0.66 & 1.46 & 75.98 & $+$0.49 \\\\\n25\t\\addlinespace\n26\tMistral-7B ($T{=}0.3$) & FOLIO & 0.53 & 1.77 & 58.33 & $-$1.47 \\\\\n27\tMistral-7B & LogiQA & 0.51 & 1.81 & 51.00 & $+$0.50 \\\\\n28\tMistral-7B (s456) & FOLIO & 0.51 & 1.82 & 55.88 & 0.00 \\\\\n29\tMistral-7B & FOLIO & 0.50 & 1.84 & 55.88 & $+$3.43 \\\\\n30\tMistral-7B (s123) & FOLIO & 0.49 & 1.88 & 54.41 & $+$2.94 \\\\\n31\tMistral-7B (s789) & FOLIO & 0.41 & 2.17 & 54.41 & $-$0.49 \\\\\n32\tMistral-7B ($T{=}0.5$) & FOLIO & 0.52 & 1.80 & 58.33 & $-$0.98 \\\\\n33\tMistral-7B (multi-T) & FOLIO & 0.48 & 1.91 & 57.84 & 0.00 \\\\\n34\tMistral-7B ($T{=}1.0$) & FOLIO & 0.48 & 1.91 & 55.39 & 0.00 \\\\\n35\tMistral-7B & PW-D5 & 0.21 & 3.62 & 39.33 & $\\mathbf{-2.83}${*} \\\\\n36\t\\bottomrule\n37\t\\end{tabular}\n38\t\\end{table}\n39\t\n40\t\n41\t\\subsection{Symbolic Confirmation Bias}\n42\t\\label{sec:confirmation-bias}\n43\t\n44\tThe correlation above manifests through a specific mechanism: self-extracted constraints systematically confirm the model's majority answer regardless of correctness. On Mistral-7B FOLIO ($n = 204$), when SICA errs, 83.3\\% of extracted constraints support the incorrect majority answer---a bias ratio of $4.97\\times$ (raw constraint counts; $4.24\\times$ when MaxSAT-weighted) compared to correct problems. Consequently, 94\\% of SICA errors overlap with SC errors: symbolic aggregation adds no independent corrective signal.\n45\t\n46\tThe causal chain is: the model generates an answer, then extracts FOL consistent with it. Constraints from majority-answer traces use trace-specific variable names (56.4\\% of cross-answer pairs share zero Z3 variables), so MAX-SAT retains nearly all, and aggregation reduces to a weighted majority vote (Eq.~\\ref{eq:degenerate}). Confirmation bias generalizes across model families: bias ratios of $4.97\\times$ (Mistral-7B), $3.93\\times$ (Qwen2.5-14B), and $2.88{\\times}^{\\dag}$ (LLaMA-3.1-8B) all significantly exceed $1.0$ ($^{\\dag}$based on 4/12 traces due to extraction failures; Table~\\ref{tab:cross-model-bias}). The oracle ceiling (\\S\\ref{sec:oracle}) confirms that 75.9\\% of the potential gain is lost to self-extraction quality.\n47\t\n48\t\n49\t\\subsection{Process-Level Independence}\n50\t\\label{sec:process-kappa}\n51\t\n52\tAnswer-level $\\kappa$ may underestimate the independence bottleneck: two traces can follow nearly identical reasoning yet reach different final answers, causing $\\kappa$ to treat them as disagreeing. We define process similarity as the mean pairwise cosine similarity of TF-IDF trace representations:\n53\t\\begin{equation}\n54\t\\label{eq:process-sim}\n55\t\\text{ProcessSim}(q) = \\frac{2}{K(K{-}1)} \\sum_{i < j} \\cos(\\mathbf{v}_i^q, \\mathbf{v}_j^q),\n56\t\\end{equation}\n57\twhere $\\mathbf{v}_i^q$ is the TF-IDF vector of trace $t_i$ for question $q$. On Mistral-7B FOLIO ($n = 204$), mean ProcessSim is $0.69$, far exceeding answer-level $\\kappa = 0.41$. The process--answer correlation is model-strength dependent: $r = -0.06$ ($p = 0.36$) on Mistral-7B, where answer diversity does not reflect reasoning diversity (Figure~\\ref{fig:process-kappa}), rising to $r = +0.24$ ($p < 0.001$) on Qwen2.5-14B. This pattern generalizes across four families (Table~\\ref{tab:cross-model-process}): the cosine--$\\kappa$ gap narrows from $0.23$ (Mistral) to $0.02$ (Qwen-14B), meaning answer-level $\\kappa$ already captures the bottleneck for strong models, while process-$\\kappa$ reveals hidden homogeneity in weaker models where answer diversity masks process convergence. In all families, interventions targeting answer diversity (temperature scaling, diverse prompting) cannot alter the underlying reasoning paths.\n58\t\n59\t\\begin{figure}[t]\n60\t\\centering\n61\t\\includegraphics[width=0.85\\columnwidth]{figures/fig3_process_kappa_scatter.pdf}\n62\t\\vspace{-2mm}\n63\t\\caption{Answer agreement vs.\\ CoT cosine similarity (Mistral-7B, FOLIO, $n{=}204$). Flat regression ($r = -0.06$, $p = 0.36$); stronger models show significant positive correlation ($r = +0.24$, Qwen-14B; Table~\\ref{tab:cross-model-process}).}\n64\t\\label{fig:process-kappa}\n65\t\\end{figure}\n66\t\n67\t\\subsection{Supporting Diagnostics}\n68\t\\label{sec:diagnostics}\n69\t\n70\tFurther diagnostics---constraint ablation, discriminativeness, entropy decomposition, and hyperparameter sensitivity---confirm the mechanism (Appendices~\\ref{app:extraction}--\\ref{app:entropy}).\n71\t\n72\t\n73\t\\subsection{Remediation Analysis}\n74\t\\label{sec:remediation}\n75\t\\label{sec:interventions}\n76\t\n77\tWe test more than twenty remediation strategies spanning prompt engineering, method-level redesign, and ten additional directions. All produce neutral or negative results.\n78\t\n79\t\\paragraph{Prompt-level interventions.} Nine strategies spanning six dimensions (Appendix Table~\\ref{tab:remediation}) all fail. Contrastive prompting reduces non-discriminative constraints from 8.2\\% to 6.0\\%, but newly discriminative constraints still mirror the majority ($\\Delta = -1.1$\\,pp). Cross-model extraction fails in both directions: weaker models yield 0\\% discriminative constraints; stronger models produce accuracy within $\\pm 1$\\,pp of self-extraction. Multi-temperature sampling ($T \\in \\{0.3, 0.7, 1.2\\}$) yields $\\Delta = 0.00$\\,pp.\n80\t\n81\t\\paragraph{Method-level interventions.} ICG ($\\Delta \\approx 0$, $p = .206$), Contrastive ($\\Delta = -9.31$\\,pp, $p < 0.01$), and Debate ($\\Delta = -18.63$/$-23.00$\\,pp, $p < 0.001$) all fail; three of six conditions reach significance, all negative (Table~\\ref{tab:method_interventions}, Appendix~\\ref{app:remediation}).\n82\t\n83\t\\paragraph{Additional directions.} Ten further experiments (code verification, iterative refinement, logprob filtering, cross-model ensembles, alternative objectives) all produce neutral-to-negative results (Appendix Table~\\ref{tab:additional_interventions}). Cross-model SICA exemplifies the pattern: reducing $\\kappa$ from 0.53 to 0.42 yields $\\Delta = -0.49$\\,pp---cross-architecture diversity is insufficient within self-extraction.\n84\t\n\n... /home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0513_3/projects/symb_invariant_consensus/docs/paper/solutions.tex:4:The analyses in \S\ref{sec:confirmation-bias}--\S\ref{sec:remediation} identify two structural causes of SICA's failure: constraints are coupled to their source answer (confirmation bias), and all traces share the same model distribution (independence bottleneck). We investigate two complementary approaches: decoupling constraints from answers within the same model (\S\ref{sec:decoupling}), and injecting an architecturally independent verification signal (\S\ref{sec:cross-arch}).
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0513_3/projects/symb_invariant_consensus/docs/paper/solutions.tex:7:\label{sec:decoupling}
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0513_3/projects/symb_invariant_consensus/docs/paper/solutions.tex:21:We test this with DeBERTa-large \citep{he2021deberta} (${\sim}$350M parameters, encoder-only), fine-tuned on MultiNLI \citep{williams2018mnli}. For each problem, DeBERTa classifies the (premise, conclusion) pair into \textsc{entailment}, \textsc{contradiction}, or \textsc{neutral}. In combo mode, the NLI verdict is integrated with SC's $K$ votes via a weight parameter $w$: each NLI-supporting vote counts $w$ times. We evaluate on Mistral-7B FOLIO ($n = 204$).
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0513_3/projects/symb_invariant_consensus/docs/paper/solutions.tex:24:[Omitted long matching line]
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0513_3/projects/symb_invariant_consensus/docs/paper/solutions.tex:30:The ${\sim}$350M encoder-only model and the 7B decoder-only LLM share no architecture, training data, or inference mechanism. DeBERTa classifies textual entailment rather than generating chain-of-thought, so it does not exhibit the symbolic confirmation bias identified in \S\ref{sec:confirmation-bias}. The two systems err on different problem subsets, satisfying the independence condition that same-model sampling cannot provide.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0513_3/projects/symb_invariant_consensus/docs/paper/introduction.tex:29:A cross-architecture proof-of-concept demonstrates that this gap is recoverable: DeBERTa-large NLI combined with SC achieves $+4.41$\,pp ($w{=}3$; McNemar $p = 0.039$ at $w{=}1$) through per-label complementarity (\S\ref{sec:cross-arch}).
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0513_3/projects/symb_invariant_consensus/docs/paper/introduction.tex:33:\item \textbf{Independence-Accuracy Trade-off and Cross-Architecture Escape.} Fleiss' $\kappa$ across four aggregation methods and three model families reveals a same-model Pareto frontier ($\kappa \geq 0.40$). Twenty-five+ remediation strategies fail; a cross-architecture NLI verifier (DeBERTa-large, $+4.41$\,pp; McNemar $p = 0.039$ at $w{=}1$) demonstrates the frontier is escapable (\S\ref{sec:remediation}, \S\ref{sec:cross-arch}).

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01C7YXbJxbB1hP3hmutHn1c1`</summary>

## Search Results

I found **1 occurrence** of "Gemma" (case-insensitive) in the .tex files:

**File:** `/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0513_3/projects/symb_invariant_consensus/docs/paper/discussion.tex`

**Line:** 4

**Full line content:**
```
Symbolic confirmation bias unifies two degradation channels: the \emph{direct} channel limits voting diversity (Eff-$K < 2$), while the \emph{indirect} channel causes MAX-SAT to degenerate to vote counting. The bias ratio gradient ($4.97\times$ Mistral $> 3.93\times$ Qwen $> 2.88\times$ LLaMA) suggests weaker models suffer more severe confirmation bias, and the cross-architecture NLI experiment (\S\ref{sec:cross-arch}) corroborates that complementary signal must come from outside the model's distribution. Stronger models show higher SICA-SC convergence (Gemma-2-7B: 99.5\% overlap, only 1/204 discordant), confirming confirmation bias intensifies with model capability---consistent with Qwen3 thinking mode degeneration ($\kappa = 0.91$). For practitioners, $C_0$ combined with SC accuracy predicts when augmentation is unlikely to help. The oracle ceiling ($+14.22$\,pp on FOLIO; \S\ref{sec:oracle}) shows that ${\sim}$76\% of the potential gain remains unrealized by self-extraction, motivating future work on trained extractors or hybrid verification methods. Whether 70B+ models shift the observed trade-off boundary is open.
```

**Context:** The match is "Gemma-2-7B" which refers to a specific model used in the paper's cross-architecture comparison experiments.
````

</details>

```text
1	\section{Discussion}
2	\label{sec:discussion}
3	
4	Symbolic confirmation bias unifies two degradation channels: the \emph{direct} channel limits voting diversity (Eff-$K < 2$), while the \emph{indirect} channel causes MAX-SAT to degenerate to vote counting. The bias ratio gradient ($4.97\times$ Mistral $> 3.93\times$ Qwen $> 2.88\times$ LLaMA) suggests weaker models suffer more severe confirmation bias, and the cross-architecture NLI experiment (\S\ref{sec:cross-arch}) corroborates that complementary signal must come from outside the model's distribution. Stronger models show higher SICA-SC convergence (Gemma-2-7B: 99.5\% overlap, only 1/204 discordant), confirming confirmation bias intensifies with model capability---consistent with Qwen3 thinking mode degeneration ($\kappa = 0.91$). For practitioners, $C_0$ combined with SC accuracy predicts when augmentation is unlikely to help. The oracle ceiling ($+14.22$\,pp on FOLIO; \S\ref{sec:oracle}) shows that ${\sim}$76\% of the potential gain remains unrealized by self-extraction, motivating future work on trained extractors or hybrid verification methods. Whether 70B+ models shift the observed trade-off boundary is open.
```

</details>

```text
41	\subsection{Symbolic Confirmation Bias}
42	\label{sec:confirmation-bias}
43	
44	The correlation above manifests through a specific mechanism: self-extracted constraints systematically confirm the model's majority answer regardless of correctness. On Mistral-7B FOLIO ($n = 204$), when SICA errs, 83.3\% of extracted constraints support the incorrect majority answer---a bias ratio of $4.97\times$ (raw constraint counts; $4.24\times$ when MaxSAT-weighted) compared to correct problems. Consequently, 94\% of SICA errors overlap with SC errors: symbolic aggregation adds no independent corrective signal.
45	
46	The causal chain is: the model generates an answer, then extracts FOL consistent with it. Constraints from majority-answer traces use trace-specific variable names (56.4\% of cross-answer pairs share zero Z3 variables), so MAX-SAT retains nearly all, and aggregation reduces to a weighted majority vote (Eq.~\ref{eq:degenerate}). Confirmation bias generalizes across model families: bias ratios of $4.97\times$ (Mistral-7B), $3.93\times$ (Qwen2.5-14B), and $2.88{\times}^{\dag}$ (LLaMA-3.1-8B) all significantly exceed $1.0$ ($^{\dag}$based on 4/12 traces due to extraction failures; Table~\ref{tab:cross-model-bias}). The oracle ceiling (\S\ref{sec:oracle}) confirms that 75.9\% of the potential gain is lost to self-extraction quality.
47	
48	
```

The gradient aligns with regime boundaries: high bias (Mistral, $4.24\\times$ weighted) combined with intermediate SC (54\\%) produces SICA's only positive effect, while low bias (LLaMA, $2.66\\times$) combined with high SC (66\\%) yields the worst degradation ($-4.41$\\,pp)---high bias leaves residual constraint diversity for reranking, but at high SC the model's consensus is already near-correct and constraints cannot improve upon it. Thinking-mode degeneration ($\\kappa = 0.91$, Qwen3) confirms the trend continues with capability. For practitioners, $C_0$ combined with SC accuracy predicts when augmentation is unlikely to help. The oracle ceiling ($+14.22$\\,pp on FOLIO; \\S\\ref{sec:oracle}) shows that ${\\sim}$76\\% of the potential gain remains unrealized by self-extraction, motivating future work on trained extractors or hybrid verification methods. Whether 70B+ models shift the observed trade-off boundary is open.",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Analysis}\n\\label{sec:analysis}\n\nThis section investigates \\emph{why} SICA fails: trace correlation (\\S\\ref{sec:effective-k}), symbolic confirmation bias (\\S\\ref{sec:confirmation-bias}), process-level independence (\\S\\ref{sec:process-kappa}), supporting diagnostics (\\S\\ref{sec:diagnostics}), and remediation strategies (\\S\\ref{sec:remediation}).\n\n\\subsection{Condorcet Effective-K}\n\\label{sec:effective-k}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:independence} reports Fleiss' $\\kappa$ and Effective-K (Eq.~\\ref{eq:effk}) across 15 conditions at $K = 12$. Among instruction-tuned models, $\\kappa$ ranges from 0.21 to 0.82, yielding $K_{\\text{eff}} = 1.20\\text{--}3.62$. The four Mistral-7B FOLIO seeds produce $\\kappa \\in [0.41, 0.51]$, yet $\\Delta$ ranges from $+3.43$ to $-0.49$\\,pp---seed-level constraint quality, not independence alone, appears to influence the variation. Two conditions are jointly necessary for positive $\\Delta$: sufficient $K_{\\text{eff}}$ ($\\leq 1.30$ never improves) and sufficient SC accuracy ($K_{\\text{eff}} = 3.62$ with SC $= 39\\%$ produces significant degradation). Cross-model validation confirms universality: all four families produce $\\kappa \\geq 0.41$ (Table~\\ref{tab:kappa_generalization}; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model-kappa}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Trace independence metrics ($K = 12$, sorted by $\\kappa$ descending). $\\kappa$: Fleiss' Kappa. $K_{\\text{eff}}$: Effective-K (Eq.~\\ref{eq:effk}). SC: Self-Consistency baseline. $\\Delta$: SICA $-$ SC (pp). {*}\\,$p < 0.05$; positive $\\Delta$ values do not reach significance. multi-T $= T \\in \\{0.3, 0.7, 1.2\\}$, 4 traces each.}\n\\label{tab:independence}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llccrr@{}}\n\\toprule\nModel & Condition & $\\kappa$ & $K_{\\text{eff}}$ & SC (\\%) & $\\Delta$ (pp) \\\\\n\\midrule\nQwen3 (think) & FOLIO & 0.91 & 1.09 & ${\\sim}$84 & ${\\sim}$0 \\\\\nQwen2.5-14B & LogiQA & 0.82 & 1.20 & 78.50 & $-$0.50 \\\\\nQwen3 & FOLIO & 0.77 & 1.26 & 84.80 & $-$0.49 \\\\\nQwen2.5-14B & FOLIO & 0.74 & 1.30 & 75.49 & $-$0.98 \\\\\nQwen2.5-7B & FOLIO & 0.66 & 1.46 & 75.98 & $+$0.49 \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nMistral-7B ($T{=}0.3$) & FOLIO & 0.53 & 1.77 & 58.33 & $-$1.47 \\\\\nMistral-7B & LogiQA & 0.51 & 1.81 & 51.00 & $+$0.50 \\\\\nMistral-7B (s456) & FOLIO & 0.51 & 1.82 & 55.88 & 0.00 \\\\\nMistral-7B & FOLIO & 0.50 & 1.84 & 55.88 & $+$3.43 \\\\\nMistral-7B (s123) & FOLIO & 0.49 & 1.88 & 54.41 & $+$2.94 \\\\\nMistral-7B (s789) & FOLIO & 0.41 & 2.17 & 54.41 & $-$0.49 \\\\\nMistral-7B ($T{=}0.5$) & FOLIO & 0.52 & 1.80 & 58.33 & $-$0.98 \\\\\nMistral-7B (multi-T) & FOLIO & 0.48 & 1.91 & 57.84 & 0.00 \\\\\nMistral-7B ($T{=}1.0$) & FOLIO & 0.48 & 1.91 & 55.39 & 0.00 \\\\\nMistral-7B & PW-D5 & 0.21 & 3.62 & 39.33 & $\\mathbf{-2.83}${*} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\n\\subsection{Symbolic Confirmation Bias}\n\\label{sec:confirmation-bias}\n\nThe correlation above manifests through a specific mechanism: self-extracted constraints systematically confirm the model's majority answer regardless of correctness. On Mistral-7B FOLIO ($n = 204$), when SICA errs, 83.3\\% of extracted constraints support the incorrect majority answer---a bias ratio of $4.97\\times$ (raw constraint counts; $4.24\\times$ when MaxSAT-weighted) compared to correct problems. Consequently, 94\\% of SICA errors overlap with SC errors: symbolic aggregation adds no independent corrective signal.\n\nThe causal chain is: the model generates an answer, then extracts FOL consistent with it. Constraints from majority-answer traces use trace-specific variable names (56.4\\% of cross-answer pairs share zero Z3 variables), so MAX-SAT retains nearly all, and aggregation reduces to a weighted majority vote (Eq.~\\ref{eq:degenerate}). Confirmation bias generalizes across model families: bias ratios of $4.97\\times$ (Mistral-7B), $3.93\\times$ (Qwen2.5-14B), and $2.88{\\times}^{\\dag}$ (LLaMA-3.1-8B) all significantly exceed $1.0$ ($^{\\dag}$based on 4/12 traces due to extraction failures; Table~\\ref{tab:cross-model-bias}). The oracle ceiling (\\S\\ref{sec:oracle}) confirms that 75.9\\% of the potential gain is lost to self-extraction quality.\n\n\n\\subsection{Process-Level Independence}\n\\label{sec:process-kappa}\n\nAnswer-level $\\kappa$ may underestimate the independence bottleneck: two traces can follow nearly identical reasoning yet reach different final answers, causing $\\kappa$ to treat them as disagreeing. We define process similarity as the mean pairwise cosine similarity of TF-IDF trace representations:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:process-sim}\n\\text{ProcessSim}(q) = \\frac{2}{K(K{-}1)} \\sum_{i < j} \\cos(\\mathbf{v}_i^q, \\mathbf{v}_j^q),\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $\\mathbf{v}_i^q$ is the TF-IDF vector of trace $t_i$ for question $q$. On Mistral-7B FOLIO ($n = 204$), mean ProcessSim is $0.69$, far exceeding answer-level $\\kappa = 0.41$. The process--answer correlation is model-strength dependent: $r = -0.06$ ($p = 0.36$) on Mistral-7B, where answer diversity does not reflect reasoning diversity (Figure~\\ref{fig:process-kappa}), rising to $r = +0.24$ ($p < 0.001$) on Qwen2.5-14B. This pattern generalizes across four families (Table~\\ref{tab:cross-model-process}): the cosine--$\\kappa$ gap narrows from $0.23$ (Mistral) to $0.02$ (Qwen-14B), meaning answer-level $\\kappa$ already captures the bottleneck for strong models, while process-$\\kappa$ reveals hidden homogeneity in weaker models where answer diversity masks process convergence. In all families, interventions targeting answer diversity (temperature scaling, diverse prompting) cannot alter the underlying reasoning paths.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.85\\columnwidth]{figures/fig3_process_kappa_scatter.pdf}\n\\vspace{-2mm}\n\\caption{Answer agreement vs.\\ CoT cosine similarity (Mistral-7B, FOLIO, $n{=}204$). Flat regression ($r = -0.06$, $p = 0.36$); stronger models show significant positive correlation ($r = +0.24$, Qwen-14B; Table~\\ref{tab:cross-model-process}).}\n\\label{fig:process-kappa}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Supporting Diagnostics}\n\\label{sec:diagnostics}\n\nFurther diagnostics---constraint ablation, discriminativeness, entropy decomposition, and hyperparameter sensitivity---confirm the mechanism (Appendices~\\ref{app:extraction}--\\ref{app:entropy}).\n\n\n\\subsection{Remediation Analysis}\n\\label{sec:remediation}\n\\label{sec:interventions}\n\nWe test more than twenty remediation strategies spanning prompt engineering, method-level redesign, and ten additional directions. All produce neutral or negative results.\n\n\\paragraph{Prompt-level interventions.} Nine strategies spanning six dimensions (Appendix Table~\\ref{tab:remediation}) all fail. Contrastive prompting reduces non-discriminative constraints from 8.2\\% to 6.0\\%, but newly discriminative constraints still mirror the majority ($\\Delta = -1.1$\\,pp). Cross-model extraction fails in both directions: weaker models yield 0\\% discriminative constraints; stronger models produce accuracy within $\\pm 1$\\,pp of self-extraction. Multi-temperature sampling ($T \\in \\{0.3, 0.7, 1.2\\}$) yields $\\Delta = 0.00$\\,pp.\n\n\\paragraph{Method-level interventions.} ICG ($\\Delta \\approx 0$, $p = .206$), Contrastive ($\\Delta = -9.31$\\,pp, $p < 0.01$), and Debate ($\\Delta = -18.63$/$-23.00$\\,pp, $p < 0.001$) all fail; three of six conditions reach significance, all negative (Table~\\ref{tab:method_interventions}, Appendix~\\ref{app:remediation}).\n\n\\paragraph{Additional directions.} Ten further experiments (code verification, iterative refinement, logprob filtering, cross-model ensembles, alternative objectives) all produce neutral-to-negative results (Appendix Table~\\ref{tab:additional_interventions}). Cross-model SICA exemplifies the pattern: reducing $\\kappa$ from 0.53 to 0.42 yields $\\Delta = -0.49$\\,pp---cross-architecture diversity is insufficient within self-extraction.\n\n\\paragraph{Independence-accuracy trade-off.} Table~\\ref{tab:kappa_generalization} compares Fleiss' $\\kappa$ across four same-model aggregation methods and three model families (Figure~\\ref{fig:kappa-accuracy}). SC ($\\kappa = 0.44$), Diverse-Prompt ($\\kappa = 0.40$), and ICG ($\\kappa = 0.47$) cluster at $\\kappa \\approx 0.40$--$0.47$; Debate reaches $\\kappa = 0.15$ but collapses accuracy by $-18.6$\\,pp. All three families cluster above $\\kappa = 0.40$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model-kappa}), forming a same-model trade-off ceiling at the 7B--32B scale tested: methods that increase independence destroy accuracy, and methods preserving accuracy cannot break the correlation floor.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Independence-accuracy trade-off (FOLIO, $n = 204$). Top: aggregation methods on Mistral-7B; bottom: SC across three model families. <details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01JDYGMH8nF63NMNkqfLdPx4`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Breaking Confirmation Bias}
2	\label{sec:solutions}
3	
4	The analyses in \S\ref{sec:confirmation-bias}--\S\ref{sec:remediation} identify two structural causes of SICA's failure: constraints are coupled to their source answer (confirmation bias), and all traces share the same model distribution (independence bottleneck). We investigate two complementary approaches: decoupling constraints from answers within the same model (\S\ref{sec:decoupling}), and injecting an architecturally independent verification signal (\S\ref{sec:cross-arch}).
5	
6	\subsection{Constraint-Answer Decoupling}
7	\label{sec:decoupling}
8	
9	Symbolic confirmation bias arises because the model generates an answer, then extracts constraints consistent with it (\S\ref{sec:confirmation-bias}). The causal chain runs through the trace conclusion: the final answer anchors extraction toward confirming that specific answer.
10	
11	We break this chain by truncating the conclusion from each reasoning trace before constraint extraction. The final answer segment (${\sim}$4.1\% of trace text) is removed so the extraction model sees only intermediate reasoning steps, not the concluded answer. All other pipeline components---deduplication, MAX-SAT solving, scoring---remain identical to standard SICA (\S\ref{sec:pipeline}).
12	
13	\emph{Results pending: conservative truncation on Mistral-7B FOLIO ($n = 204$), intermediate accuracy 64.3\% at 70/204 problems processed.}
14	
15	\subsection{Cross-Architecture NLI Verification}
16	\label{sec:cross-arch}
17	
18	The oracle gap (\S\ref{sec:oracle}) shows that the MAX-SAT mechanism is sound given quality constraints; twenty-five same-model strategies fail to provide them (\S\ref{sec:remediation}). The corrective signal must come from outside the reasoning model's distribution.
19	
20	\paragraph{Method.}
21	We test this with DeBERTa-large \citep{he2021deberta} (${\sim}$350M parameters, encoder-only), fine-tuned on MultiNLI \citep{williams2018mnli}. For each problem, DeBERTa classifies the (premise, conclusion) pair into \textsc{entailment}, \textsc{contradiction}, or \textsc{neutral}. In combo mode, the NLI verdict is integrated with SC's $K$ votes via a weight parameter $w$: each NLI-supporting vote counts $w$ times. We evaluate on Mistral-7B FOLIO ($n = 204$).
22	
23	\paragraph{Main result.}
24	DeBERTa NLI alone achieves 49.51\%, below SC's 54.41\% baseline. Combo mode at $w{=}3$ (NLI receives 3 of 15 total votes) reaches 58.82\% ($\Delta = +4.41$\,pp). Statistical significance is established at the most conservative weight: $w{=}1$ yields $+3.43$\,pp with McNemar exact $p = 0.039$ (discordant pairs: $b{=}1$, $c{=}8$; 95\% bootstrap CI $[0.98, 6.37]$\,pp). The effect is monotonically increasing with weight, but higher values risk dataset-specific overfitting; we adopt $w{=}3$ as the primary operating point (20\% voting share).
25	
26	\paragraph{Per-label complementarity.}
27	The gain is not uniform across labels. On \textsc{False} problems, NLI achieves 71.43\% compared to SC's 38.10\% ($+33$\,pp), because DeBERTa's contradiction detection directly identifies negated conclusions that the LLM's chain-of-thought reasoning systematically mishandles. On \textsc{Unknown} problems, the pattern reverses: SC reaches 68.12\% while NLI drops to 18.84\% ($+49$\,pp for SC), because ``neutral'' in NLI does not map cleanly to the logical \textsc{Unknown} label. The combo succeeds because these strengths are complementary. When both systems agree (45.1\% of problems), accuracy reaches 68.48\% ($+14.07$\,pp over SC), confirming that agreement between architecturally independent systems serves as a high-precision signal.
28	
29	\paragraph{Why cross-architecture verification works.}
30	The ${\sim}$350M encoder-only model and the 7B decoder-only LLM share no architecture, training data, or inference mechanism. DeBERTa classifies textual entailment rather than generating chain-of-thought, so it does not exhibit the symbolic confirmation bias identified in \S\ref{sec:confirmation-bias}. The two systems err on different problem subsets, satisfying the independence condition that same-model sampling cannot provide.
31	
```

</details>

    "newString": "DeBERTa-large, $+3.43$\\,pp, $p = 0.039$",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nTest-time computation scaling through multiple reasoning samples has emerged as a practical alternative to scaling model parameters \\citep{snell2025scaling, guo2025deepseekr1}.\nThe dominant aggregation strategy, Self-Consistency (SC) \\citep{wang2023sc}, samples $K$ chain-of-thought traces and selects the majority answer.\nSC is simple, model-agnostic, and effective---but it is hitting a ceiling.\n\\citet{loo2025reevaluating} demonstrate that gains from increasing $K$ plateau and even decline for strong models.\nThe gap between SC's theoretical promise (independent correct voters converge to truth) and its practical behavior (saturating gains) points to a structural mismatch: same-model traces carry correlated errors, and majority voting has no mechanism to correct shared bias.\n\nConfidence-weighted \\citep{taubenfeld2025cisc} and universal \\citep{chen2024usc} extensions alleviate this to a degree, but the core limitation persists.\nRecent neuro-symbolic methods---Logic-LM \\citep{pan2023logiclm}, LINC \\citep{olausson2023linc}, DyLoS \\citep{xu2026dylos}, Aristotle \\citep{li2025aristotle}, FoVer \\citep{wang2025fover}, LogicAgent \\citep{luo2025logicagent}---each report gains on logical reasoning, but introduce components beyond the reasoning model: trained verifiers, solver selection, or external knowledge.\nA simpler question precedes these: can the \\emph{same} model that generates reasoning traces also extract useful formal constraints from them?\nSelf-extraction would be a zero-cost upgrade requiring no additional model, training data, or external prover.\nYet no prior work has systematically evaluated whether self-extracted constraints are useful for aggregation.\n\nWe ground our analysis in the Condorcet Jury Theorem (CJT) \\citep{condorcet1785}: if $K$ independent voters each answer correctly with probability $p > 0.5$, the majority converges to truth \\citep{austensmith1996}.\nWhen independence fails \\citep{ladha1992correlated}, additional samples carry diminishing information.\nMeasuring inter-trace agreement with Fleiss' $\\kappa$ \\citep{fleiss1971kappa}, we find $\\kappa = 0.21$--$0.82$ across instruction-tuned conditions, yielding Effective-$K = 1.20$--$3.62$ for nominal $K = 12$: traces beyond a dozen add negligible independent information (\\S\\ref{sec:effective-k}).\nThe bottleneck runs deeper than answer-level agreement suggests.\nChain-of-thought cosine similarity ($0.69$) far exceeds answer-level $\\kappa$ ($0.41$), revealing that traces share reasoning processes even when they diverge on final answers (\\S\\ref{sec:process-kappa}).\nWhen the same model extracts FOL constraints from these correlated traces, the constraints exhibit \\emph{symbolic confirmation bias}: on problems where the model errs, 83.3\\% of self-extracted constraints support the wrong majority answer (bias ratio $4.97\\times$), and 94\\% of errors overlap with SC (\\S\\ref{sec:confirmation-bias}).\nSelf-extraction cannot supply the independent signal that CJT requires.\n\nWe study this through Symbolic Invariant Consensus Aggregation (SICA): extract FOL constraints from each of $K$ reasoning traces, deduplicate by syntactic equivalence, and select the answer best supported by a weighted partial MAX-SAT solution via Z3 \\citep{demoura2008z3} (\\S\\ref{sec:pipeline}; Figure~\\ref{fig:pipeline}).\nWe evaluate SICA across six models (7B--32B), three reasoning domains (FOLIO \\citep{han2022folio}, ProofWriter \\citep{tafjord2021proofwriter}, LogiQA \\citep{liu2020logiqa}), and over 3,000 condition-evaluations (${\\sim}$1,250 unique instances) with four-seed replication (Table~\\ref{tab:main_results}).\nMore than twenty-five remediation strategies---spanning prompt engineering, method-level redesign, and ten additional directions targeting the independence bottleneck---all produce neutral or negative results, confirming the bottleneck is structural at the 7B--32B scale tested.\n\nOracle controls confirm the aggregation mechanism is sound: gold FOL yields $+14.22$\\,pp on FOLIO and $+11.50$\\,pp on ProofWriter ($p < 0.001$), yet self-extraction captures only 24\\% of this ceiling (\\S\\ref{sec:oracle}).\nThe central finding is a fundamental independence-accuracy trade-off: Debate reduces $\\kappa$ from 0.44 to 0.15 (tripling Eff-$K$) but collapses accuracy by $-18.6$\\,pp; SC, Diverse-Prompt, and ICG all cluster at $\\kappa \\approx 0.40$--$0.47$, forming a same-model ceiling that holds across three model families (\\S\\ref{sec:remediation}).\nA cross-architecture proof-of-concept demonstrates that this gap is recoverable: DeBERTa-large NLI combined with SC achieves $+3.43$\\,pp ($p = 0.039$, McNemar exact test) through per-label complementarity (\\S\\ref{sec:cross-arch}).\n\nOur contributions are:\n\\begin{enumerate}[nosep,leftmargin=*]\n\\item \\textbf{Independence-Accuracy Trade-off and Cross-Architecture Escape.} Fleiss' $\\kappa$ across four aggregation methods and three model families reveals a same-model Pareto frontier ($\\kappa \\geq 0.40$). Twenty-five+ remediation strategies fail; a cross-architecture NLI verifier (DeBERTa-large, $+4.41$\\,pp; McNemar $p = 0.039$ at $w{=}1$) demonstrates the frontier is escapable (\\S\\ref{sec:remediation}, \\S\\ref{sec:cross-arch}).\n\n\\item \\textbf{Symbolic Confirmation Bias.} On incorrect problems, 83.3\\% of self-extracted constraints support the wrong majority answer (bias ratio $4.97\\times$), and 94\\% of errors overlap with SC (\\S\\ref{sec:confirmation-bias}).\n\n\\item \\textbf{Process-Level Independence.} Across four model families, CoT cosine similarity ($0.69$--$0.79$) far exceeds answer-level $\\kappa$ ($0.45$--$0.74$); the gap intensifies with model capability ($r = 0.23$, $p \\leq 0.001$ for Qwen vs.\\ $r \\approx 0$ for Mistral/LLaMA), showing reasoning homogeneity is architecture-independent (\\S\\ref{sec:process-kappa}).\n\n\\item \\textbf{Regime-Dependent Diagnostics.} Three failure modes create regime boundaries; $C_0$ combined with SC accuracy provides a zero-cost diagnostic predicting when symbolic augmentation is unlikely to help (\\S\\ref{sec:main-results}).\n\\end{enumerate}\n",

The effect increases with weight: $w{=}3$ yields 58.82\\% ($+4.41$\\,pp) and $w{=}5$ reaches 60.29\\% ($+5.88$\\,pp); McNemar tests for these configurations are not yet available. Note that this cross-architecture approach operates outside the same-model self-extraction scope of SICA (\\S\\ref{sec:pipeline}), serving as a proof-of-concept for external signal integration.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Breaking Confirmation Bias}\n\\label{sec:solutions}\n\nThe analyses in \\S\\ref{sec:confirmation-bias}--\\S\\ref{sec:remediation} identify two structural causes of SICA's failure: constraints are coupled to their source answer (confirmation bias), and all traces share the same model distribution (independence bottleneck). We investigate two complementary approaches: decoupling constraints from answers within the same model (\\S\\ref{sec:decoupling}), and injecting an architecturally independent verification signal (\\S\\ref{sec:cross-arch}).\n\n\\subsection{Constraint-Answer Decoupling}\n\\label{sec:decoupling}\n\nSymbolic confirmation bias arises because the model generates an answer, then extracts constraints consistent with it (\\S\\ref{sec:confirmation-bias}). The causal chain runs through the trace conclusion: the final answer anchors extraction toward confirming that specific answer.\n\nWe break this chain by truncating the conclusion from each reasoning trace before constraint extraction. The final answer segment (${\\sim}$4.1\\% of trace text) is removed so the extraction model sees only intermediate reasoning steps, not the concluded answer. All other pipeline components---deduplication, MAX-SAT solving, scoring---remain identical to standard SICA (\\S\\ref{sec:pipeline}).\n\n\\emph{Results pending: conservative truncation on Mistral-7B FOLIO ($n = 204$), intermediate accuracy 64.3\\% at 70/204 problems processed.}\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Architecture NLI Verification}\n\\label{sec:cross-arch}\n\nThe oracle gap (\\S\\ref{sec:oracle}) shows that the MAX-SAT mechanism is sound given quality constraints; twenty-five same-model strategies fail to provide them (\\S\\ref{sec:remediation}). The corrective signal must come from outside the reasoning model's distribution.\n\n\\paragraph{Method.}\nWe test this with DeBERTa-large \\citep{he2021deberta} (${\\sim}$350M parameters, encoder-only), fine-tuned on MultiNLI \\citep{williams2018mnli}. For each problem, DeBERTa classifies the (premise, conclusion) pair into \\textsc{entailment}, \\textsc{contradiction}, or \\textsc{neutral}. In combo mode, the NLI verdict is integrated with SC's $K$ votes via a weight parameter $w$: each NLI-supporting vote counts $w$ times. We evaluate on Mistral-7B FOLIO ($n = 204$).\n\n\\paragraph{Main result.}\nDeBERTa NLI alone achieves 49.51\\%, below SC's 54.41\\% baseline. Combo mode at $w{=}3$ (NLI receives 3 of 15 total votes) reaches 58.82\\% ($\\Delta = +4.41$\\,pp). Statistical significance is established at the most conservative weight: $w{=}1$ yields $+3.43$\\,pp with McNemar exact $p = 0.039$ (discordant pairs: $b{=}1$, $c{=}8$; 95\\% bootstrap CI $[0.98, 6.37]$\\,pp). The effect is monotonically increasing with weight, but higher values risk dataset-specific overfitting; we adopt $w{=}3$ as the primary operating point (20\\% voting share).\n\n\\paragraph{Per-label complementarity.}\nThe gain is not uniform across labels. On \\textsc{False} problems, NLI achieves 71.43\\% compared to SC's 38.10\\% ($+33$\\,pp), because DeBERTa's contradiction detection directly identifies negated conclusions that the LLM's chain-of-thought reasoning systematically mishandles. On \\textsc{Unknown} problems, the pattern reverses: SC reaches 68.12\\% while NLI drops to 18.84\\% ($+49$\\,pp for SC), because ``neutral'' in NLI does not map cleanly to the logical \\textsc{Unknown} label. The combo succeeds because these strengths are complementary. When both systems agree (45.1\\% of problems), accuracy reaches 68.48\\% ($+14.07$\\,pp over SC), confirming that agreement between architecturally independent systems serves as a high-precision signal.\n\n\\paragraph{Why cross-architecture verification works.}\nThe ${\\sim}$350M encoder-only model and the 7B decoder-only LLM share no architecture, training data, or inference mechanism. DeBERTa classifies textual entailment rather than generating chain-of-thought, so it does not exhibit the symbolic confirmation bias identified in \\S\\ref{sec:confirmation-bias}. The two systems err on different problem subsets, satisfying the independence condition that same-model sampling cannot provide.\n",

    "originalFile": "Self-Consistency (SC) majority voting over chain-of-thought traces saturates as sample count grows because same-model traces are correlated voters.\nWe investigate whether symbolic constraint extraction can break this plateau through Symbolic Invariant Consensus Aggregation (SICA), which extracts first-order logic (FOL) constraints from reasoning traces and selects answers via weighted partial MAX-SAT.\nEvaluating six models (7B--32B) across three reasoning domains with over 3,000 condition-evaluations (${\\sim}$1,250 unique instances), we find that self-extracted constraints fail to surpass majority voting: none of fifteen non-degenerate conditions shows a significant positive effect ($p < 0.05$), while two conditions show significant degradation.\nWe identify \\emph{symbolic confirmation bias} as the root cause: on incorrect problems, 83.3\\% of self-extracted constraints support the wrong majority answer (bias ratio $4.97\\times$), and 94\\% of errors overlap with SC.\nProcess-level analysis reveals a deeper bottleneck: chain-of-thought cosine similarity ($0.69$) far exceeds answer-level Fleiss' $\\kappa$ ($0.41$), indicating that traces share reasoning processes even when they diverge on answers.\nMore than twenty-five same-model remediation strategies---spanning prompt engineering, method-level redesign, and ten additional directions---all fail, revealing a fundamental independence-accuracy trade-off: methods that increase trace independence destroy accuracy (Debate: $\\kappa = 0.15$, $-18.6$\\,pp), while methods preserving accuracy cannot break the correlation floor ($\\kappa \\geq 0.40$ across three model families).\nOracle controls confirm the aggregation mechanism is sound ($+14.22$\\,pp FOLIO, $+11.50$\\,pp ProofWriter, $p < 0.001$); self-extraction captures only 24\\% of this ceiling.\nHowever, a cross-architecture proof-of-concept---DeBERTa-large NLI combined with SC---achieves $+4.41$\\,pp ($w{=}3$; McNemar $p = 0.039$ at $w{=}1$) through per-label complementarity, demonstrating that the oracle gap is practically recoverable when verification signal comes from outside the reasoning model's distribution.\n",

{
  "recorded_role": "user",
  "parentUuid": "c8da8bbb-a13c-4413-ae86-2a55b2fcd3b6",

    "oldString": "achieves $+4.41$\\,pp ($w{=}3$; McNemar $p = 0.039$ at $w{=}1$) through per-label complementarity",
    "newString": "achieves $+3.43$\\,pp ($p = 0.039$, McNemar exact test) through per-label complementarity",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nTest-time computation scaling through multiple reasoning samples has emerged as a practical alternative to scaling model parameters \\citep{snell2025scaling, guo2025deepseekr1}.\nThe dominant aggregation strategy, Self-Consistency (SC) \\citep{wang2023sc}, samples $K$ chain-of-thought traces and selects the majority answer.\nSC is simple, model-agnostic, and effective---but it is hitting a ceiling.\n\\citet{loo2025reevaluating} demonstrate that gains from increasing $K$ plateau and even decline for strong models.\nThe gap between SC's theoretical promise (independent correct voters converge to truth) and its practical behavior (saturating gains) points to a structural mismatch: same-model traces carry correlated errors, and majority voting has no mechanism to correct shared bias.\n\nConfidence-weighted \\citep{taubenfeld2025cisc} and universal \\citep{chen2024usc} extensions alleviate this to a degree, but the core limitation persists.\nRecent neuro-symbolic methods---Logic-LM \\citep{pan2023logiclm}, LINC \\citep{olausson2023linc}, DyLoS \\citep{xu2026dylos}, Aristotle \\citep{li2025aristotle}, FoVer \\citep{wang2025fover}, LogicAgent \\citep{luo2025logicagent}---each report gains on logical reasoning, but introduce components beyond the reasoning model: trained verifiers, solver selection, or external knowledge.\nA simpler question precedes these: can the \\emph{same} model that generates reasoning traces also extract useful formal constraints from them?\nSelf-extraction would be a zero-cost upgrade requiring no additional model, training data, or external prover.\nYet no prior work has systematically evaluated whether self-extracted constraints are useful for aggregation.\n\nWe ground our analysis in the Condorcet Jury Theorem (CJT) \\citep{condorcet1785}: if $K$ independent voters each answer correctly with probability $p > 0.5$, the majority converges to truth \\citep{austensmith1996}.\nWhen independence fails \\citep{ladha1992correlated}, additional samples carry diminishing information.\nMeasuring inter-trace agreement with Fleiss' $\\kappa$ \\citep{fleiss1971kappa}, we find $\\kappa = 0.21$--$0.82$ across instruction-tuned conditions, yielding Effective-$K = 1.20$--$3.62$ for nominal $K = 12$: traces beyond a dozen add negligible independent information (\\S\\ref{sec:effective-k}).\nThe bottleneck runs deeper than answer-level agreement suggests.\nChain-of-thought cosine similarity ($0.69$) far exceeds answer-level $\\kappa$ ($0.41$), revealing that traces share reasoning processes even when they diverge on final answers (\\S\\ref{sec:process-kappa}).\nWhen the same model extracts FOL constraints from these correlated traces, the constraints exhibit \\emph{symbolic confirmation bias}: on problems where the model errs, 83.3\\% of self-extracted constraints support the wrong majority answer (bias ratio $4.97\\times$), and 94\\% of errors overlap with SC (\\S\\ref{sec:confirmation-bias}).\nSelf-extraction cannot supply the independent signal that CJT requires.\n\nWe study this through Symbolic Invariant Consensus Aggregation (SICA): extract FOL constraints from each of $K$ reasoning traces, deduplicate by syntactic equivalence, and select the answer best supported by a weighted partial MAX-SAT solution via Z3 \\citep{demoura2008z3} (\\S\\ref{sec:pipeline}; Figure~\\ref{fig:pipeline}).\nWe evaluate SICA across six models (7B--32B), three reasoning domains (FOLIO \\citep{han2022folio}, ProofWriter \\citep{tafjord2021proofwriter}, LogiQA \\citep{liu2020logiqa}), and over 3,000 condition-evaluations (${\\sim}$1,250 unique instances) with four-seed replication (Table~\\ref{tab:main_results}).\nMore than twenty-five remediation strategies---spanning prompt engineering, method-level redesign, and ten additional directions targeting the independence bottleneck---all produce neutral or negative results, confirming the bottleneck is structural at the 7B--32B scale tested.\n\nOracle controls confirm the aggregation mechanism is sound: gold FOL yields $+14.22$\\,pp on FOLIO and $+11.50$\\,pp on ProofWriter ($p < 0.001$), yet self-extraction captures only 24\\% of this ceiling (\\S\\ref{sec:oracle}).\nThe central finding is a fundamental independence-accuracy trade-off: Debate reduces $\\kappa$ from 0.44 to 0.15 (tripling Eff-$K$) but collapses accuracy by $-18.6$\\,pp; SC, Diverse-Prompt, and ICG all cluster at $\\kappa \\approx 0.40$--$0.47$, forming a same-model ceiling that holds across three model families (\\S\\ref{sec:remediation}).\nA cross-architecture proof-of-concept demonstrates that this gap is recoverable: DeBERTa-large NLI combined with SC achieves $+4.41$\\,pp ($w{=}3$; McNemar $p = 0.039$ at $w{=}1$) through per-label complementarity (\\S\\ref{sec:cross-arch}).\n\nOur contributions are:\n\\begin{enumerate}[nosep,leftmargin=*]\n\\item \\textbf{Independence-Accuracy Trade-off and Cross-Architecture Escape.} Fleiss' $\\kappa$ across four aggregation methods and three model families reveals a same-model Pareto frontier ($\\kappa \\geq 0.40$). Twenty-five+ remediation strategies fail; a cross-architecture NLI verifier (DeBERTa-large, $+4.41$\\,pp; McNemar $p = 0.039$ at $w{=}1$) demonstrates the frontier is escapable (\\S\\ref{sec:remediation}, \\S\\ref{sec:cross-arch}).\n\n\\item \\textbf{Symbolic Confirmation Bias.} On incorrect problems, 83.3\\% of self-extracted constraints support the wrong majority answer (bias ratio $4.97\\times$), and 94\\% of errors overlap with SC (\\S\\ref{sec:confirmation-bias}).\n\n\\item \\textbf{Process-Level Independence.} Across four model families, CoT cosine similarity ($0.69$--$0.79$) far exceeds answer-level $\\kappa$ ($0.45$--$0.74$); the gap intensifies with model capability ($r = 0.23$, $p \\leq 0.001$ for Qwen vs.\\ $r \\approx 0$ for Mistral/LLaMA), showing reasoning homogeneity is architecture-independent (\\S\\ref{sec:process-kappa}).\n\n\\item \\textbf{Regime-Dependent Diagnostics.} Three failure modes create regime boundaries; $C_0$ combined with SC accuracy provides a zero-cost diagnostic predicting when symbolic augmentation is unlikely to help (\\S\\ref{sec:main-results}).\n\\end{enumerate}\n",

          " The central finding is a fundamental independence-accuracy trade-off: Debate reduces $\\kappa$ from 0.44 to 0.15 (tripling Eff-$K$) but collapses accuracy by $-18.6$\\,pp; SC, Diverse-Prompt, and ICG all cluster at $\\kappa \\approx 0.40$--$0.47$, forming a same-model ceiling that holds across three model families (\\S\\ref{sec:remediation}).",
          "-A cross-architecture proof-of-concept demonstrates that this gap is recoverable: DeBERTa-large NLI combined with SC achieves $+4.41$\\,pp ($w{=}3$; McNemar $p = 0.039$ at $w{=}1$) through per-label complementarity (\\S\\ref{sec:cross-arch}).",
          "+A cross-architecture proof-of-concept demonstrates that this gap is recoverable: DeBERTa-large NLI combined with SC achieves $+3.43$\\,pp ($p = 0.039$, McNemar exact test) through per-label complementarity (\\S\\ref{sec:cross-arch}).",

_source line 11848 · 2026-05-20T13:38:39.845Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_016JMK73e72mkMwYXnNkywgF`</summary>

```text
23	\paragraph{Main result.}
24	DeBERTa NLI alone achieves 49.51\%, below SC's 54.41\% baseline. Combo mode at $w{=}1$ (NLI as 13th voter) reaches 57.84\% ($\Delta = +3.43$\,pp, McNemar exact $p = 0.039$; discordant pairs: $b{=}1$, $c{=}8$; 95\% bootstrap CI $[0.59, 6.28]$\,pp). The effect increases with weight: $w{=}3$ yields 58.82\% ($+4.41$\,pp) and $w{=}5$ reaches 60.29\% ($+5.88$\,pp); McNemar tests for these configurations are not yet available. Higher weights risk dataset-specific overfitting; we report $w{=}1$ as the primary result. Note that this cross-architecture approach operates outside the same-model self-extraction scope of SICA (\S\ref{sec:pipeline}), serving as a proof-of-concept for external signal integration.
25	
```

</details>

### Tool result

_source line 11866 · 2026-05-20T13:39:18.785Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_018mZwRhtE2arP2i3ArPSkCX`</summary>

```text
23	\paragraph{Main result.}
24	DeBERTa NLI alone achieves 49.51\%, below SC's 54.41\% baseline. Combo mode at $w{=}1$ (NLI as 13th voter) reaches 57.84\% ($\Delta = +3.43$\,pp, McNemar exact $p = 0.039$; discordant pairs: $b{=}1$, $c{=}8$; 95\% bootstrap CI $[0.59, 6.28]$\,pp). The effect increases with weight: $w{=}3$ yields 58.82\% ($+4.41$\,pp) and $w{=}5$ reaches 60.29\% ($+5.88$\,pp); McNemar tests for these configurations are not yet available. Higher weights risk dataset-specific overfitting; we report $w{=}1$ as the primary result. Note that this cross-architecture approach operates outside the same-model self-extraction scope of SICA (\S\ref{sec:pipeline}), serving as a proof-of-concept for external signal integration.
25	
```

</details>

    "originalFile": "\\section{Breaking Confirmation Bias}\n\\label{sec:solutions}\n\nThe analyses in \\S\\ref{sec:confirmation-bias}--\\S\\ref{sec:remediation} identify two structural causes of SICA's failure: constraints are coupled to their source answer (confirmation bias), and all traces share the same model distribution (independence bottleneck). We investigate two complementary approaches: decoupling constraints from answers within the same model (\\S\\ref{sec:decoupling}), and injecting an architecturally independent verification signal (\\S\\ref{sec:cross-arch}).\n\n\\subsection{Constraint-Answer Decoupling}\n\\label{sec:decoupling}\n\nSymbolic confirmation bias arises because the model generates an answer, then extracts constraints consistent with it (\\S\\ref{sec:confirmation-bias}). The causal chain runs through the trace conclusion: the final answer anchors extraction toward confirming that specific answer.\n\nWe break this chain by truncating the conclusion from each reasoning trace before constraint extraction. The final answer segment (${\\sim}$4.1\\% of trace text) is removed so the extraction model sees only intermediate reasoning steps, not the concluded answer. All other pipeline components---deduplication, MAX-SAT solving, scoring---remain identical to standard SICA (\\S\\ref{sec:pipeline}).\n\n\\emph{Results pending: conservative truncation on Mistral-7B FOLIO ($n = 204$), intermediate accuracy 64.3\\% at 70/204 problems processed.}\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Architecture NLI Verification}\n\\label{sec:cross-arch}\n\nThe oracle gap (\\S\\ref{sec:oracle}) shows that the MAX-SAT mechanism is sound given quality constraints; twenty-five same-model strategies fail to provide them (\\S\\ref{sec:remediation}). The corrective signal must come from outside the reasoning model's distribution.\n\n\\paragraph{Method.}\nWe test this with DeBERTa-large \\citep{he2021deberta} (406M parameters, encoder-only), fine-tuned on MultiNLI \\citep{williams2018mnli}. For each problem, DeBERTa classifies the (premise, conclusion) pair into \\textsc{entailment}, \\textsc{contradiction}, or \\textsc{neutral}. In combo mode, the NLI verdict is integrated with SC's $K$ votes via a weight parameter $w$: each NLI-supporting vote counts $w$ times. We evaluate on Mistral-7B FOLIO ($n = 204$).\n\n\\paragraph{Main result.}\nDeBERTa NLI alone achieves 49.51\\%, below SC's 54.41\\% baseline. Combo mode at $w{=}1$ (NLI as 13th voter) reaches 57.84\\% ($\\Delta = +3.43$\\,pp, McNemar exact $p = 0.039$; discordant pairs: $b{=}1$, $c{=}8$; 95\\% bootstrap CI $[0.59, 6.28]$\\,pp). The effect increases with weight: $w{=}3$ yields 58.82\\% ($+4.41$\\,pp) and $w{=}5$ reaches 60.29\\% ($+5.88$\\,pp); McNemar tests for these configurations are not yet available. Higher weights risk dataset-specific overfitting; we report $w{=}1$ as the primary result. Note that this cross-architecture approach operates outside the same-model self-extraction scope of SICA (\\S\\ref{sec:pipeline}), serving as a proof-of-concept for external signal integration.\n\n\\paragraph{Per-label complementarity.}\nThe gain is not uniform across labels. On \\textsc{False} problems, NLI achieves 71.43\\% compared to SC's 38.10\\% ($+33$\\,pp), because DeBERTa's contradiction detection directly identifies negated conclusions that the LLM's chain-of-thought reasoning systematically mishandles. On \\textsc{Unknown} problems, the pattern reverses: SC reaches 68.12\\% while NLI drops to 18.84\\% ($+49$\\,pp for SC), because ``neutral'' in NLI does not map cleanly to the logical \\textsc{Unknown} label. The combo succeeds because these strengths are complementary. When both systems agree (45.1\\% of problems), accuracy reaches 68.48\\% ($+14.07$\\,pp over SC), confirming that agreement between architecturally independent systems serves as a high-precision signal.\n\n\\paragraph{Why cross-architecture verification works.}\nThe 406M encoder-only model and the 7B decoder-only LLM share no architecture, training data, or inference mechanism. DeBERTa classifies textual entailment rather than generating chain-of-thought, so it does not exhibit the symbolic confirmation bias identified in \\S\\ref{sec:confirmation-bias}. The two systems err on different problem subsets, satisfying the independence condition that same-model sampling cannot provide.\n",

{

  "isSidechain": false,

    "type": "edited_text_file",
    "filename": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0513_3/projects/symb_invariant_consensus/docs/paper/introduction.tex",
    "snippet": "1\t\\section{Introduction}\\label{sec:introduction}\n2\t\n3\tTest-time computation scaling through multiple reasoning samples has emerged as a practical alternative to scaling model parameters \\citep{snell2025scaling, guo2025deepseekr1}.\n4\tThe dominant aggregation strategy, Self-Consistency (SC) \\citep{wang2023sc}, samples $K$ chain-of-thought traces and selects the majority answer.\n5\tSC is simple, model-agnostic, and effective---but it is hitting a ceiling.\n6\t\\citet{loo2025reevaluating} demonstrate that gains from increasing $K$ plateau and even decline for strong models.\n7\tThe gap between SC's theoretical promise (independent correct voters converge to truth) and its practical behavior (saturating gains) points to a structural mismatch: same-model traces carry correlated errors, and majority voting has no mechanism to correct shared bias.\n8\t\n9\tConfidence-weighted \\citep{taubenfeld2025cisc} and universal \\citep{chen2024usc} extensions alleviate this to a degree, but the core limitation persists.\n10\tRecent neuro-symbolic methods---Logic-LM \\citep{pan2023logiclm}, LINC \\citep{olausson2023linc}, DyLoS \\citep{xu2026dylos}, Aristotle \\citep{li2025aristotle}, FoVer \\citep{wang2025fover}, LogicAgent \\citep{luo2025logicagent}---each report gains on logical reasoning, but introduce components beyond the reasoning model: trained verifiers, solver selection, or external knowledge.\n11\tA simpler question precedes these: can the \\emph{same} model that generates reasoning traces also extract useful formal constraints from them?\n12\tSelf-extraction would be a zero-cost upgrade requiring no additional model, training data, or external prover.\n13\tYet no prior work has systematically evaluated whether self-extracted constraints are useful for aggregation.\n14\t\n15\tWe ground our analysis in the Condorcet Jury Theorem (CJT) \\citep{condorcet1785}: if $K$ independent voters each answer correctly with probability $p > 0.5$, the majority converges to truth \\citep{austensmith1996}.\n16\tWhen independence fails \\citep{ladha1992correlated}, additional samples carry diminishing information.\n17\tMeasuring inter-trace agreement with Fleiss' $\\kappa$ \\citep{fleiss1971kappa}, we find $\\kappa = 0.21$--$0.82$ across instruction-tuned conditions, yielding Effective-$K = 1.20$--$3.62$ for nominal $K = 12$: traces beyond a dozen add negligible independent information (\\S\\ref{sec:effective-k}).\n18\tThe bottleneck runs deeper than answer-level agreement suggests.\n19\tChain-of-thought cosine similarity ($0.69$) far exceeds answer-level $\\kappa$ ($0.41$), revealing that traces share reasoning processes even when they diverge on final answers (\\S\\ref{sec:process-kappa}).\n20\tWhen the same model extracts FOL constraints from these correlated traces, the constraints exhibit \\emph{symbolic confirmation bias}: on problems where the model errs, 83.3\\% of self-extracted constraints support the wrong majority answer (bias ratio $4.97\\times$), and 94\\% of errors overlap with SC (\\S\\ref{sec:confirmation-bias}).\n21\tSelf-extraction cannot supply the independent signal that CJT requires.\n22\t\n23\tWe study this through Symbolic Invariant Consensus Aggregation (SICA): extract FOL constraints from each of $K$ reasoning traces, deduplicate by syntactic equivalence, and select the answer best supported by a weighted partial MAX-SAT solution via Z3 \\citep{demoura2008z3} (\\S\\ref{sec:pipeline}; Figure~\\ref{fig:pipeline}).\n24\tWe evaluate SICA across six models (7B--32B), three reasoning domains (FOLIO \\citep{han2022folio}, ProofWriter \\citep{tafjord2021proofwriter}, LogiQA \\citep{liu2020logiqa}), and over 3,000 condition-evaluations (${\\sim}$1,250 unique instances) with four-seed replication (Table~\\ref{tab:main_results}).\n25\tMore than twenty remediation strategies spanning fifteen distinct families---from prompt engineering through method-level redesign to ten additional directions targeting the independence bottleneck---all produce neutral or negative results, confirming the bottleneck is persistent at the 7B--32B scale tested.\n26\t\n27\tOracle controls confirm the aggregation mechanism is sound: same-model gold FOL yields $+29.90$\\,pp (Mistral-7B) and $+14.22$\\,pp (Qwen-14B) on FOLIO ($p < 0.001$), yet self-extraction captures only ${\\sim}$12\\% of the same-model ceiling (\\S\\ref{sec:oracle}).\n28\tThe central finding is a persistent independence-accuracy trade-off: Debate reduces $\\kappa$ from 0.44 to 0.15 (tripling Eff-$K$) but collapses accuracy by $-18.6$\\,pp; SC, Diverse-Prompt, and ICG all cluster at $\\kappa \\approx 0.40$--$0.47$, forming a same-model ceiling that holds across three model families (\\S\\ref{sec:remediation}).\n29\tA cross-architecture proof-of-concept demonstrates that this gap is recoverable: DeBERTa-large NLI combined with SC achieves $+3.43$\\,pp ($p = 0.039$, McNemar exact test) through per-label complementarity (\\S\\ref{sec:cross-arch}).\n30\t\n31\tOur contributions are:\n32\t\\begin{enumerate}[nosep,leftmargin=*]\n33\t\\item \\textbf{Independence-Accuracy Trade-off and Cross-Architecture Escape.} Fleiss' $\\kappa$ across four aggregation methods and three model families reveals a same-model Pareto frontier ($\\kappa \\geq 0.40$). Twenty-plus remediation strategies spanning fifteen families fail; a cross-architecture NLI verifier (DeBERTa-large, $+3.43$\\,pp, $p = 0.039$) demonstrates the frontier is escapable (\\S\\ref{sec:remediation}, \\S\\ref{sec:cross-arch}).\n34\t\n35\t\\item \\textbf{Symbolic Confirmation Bias.} On incorrect problems, 83.3\\% of self-extracted constraints support the wrong majority answer (bias ratio $4.97\\times$), and 94\\% of errors overlap with SC (\\S\\ref{sec:confirmation-bias}).\n36\t\n37\t\\item \\textbf{Process-Level Independence.} Across four model families, CoT cosine similarity ($0.69$--$0.79$) far exceeds answer-level $\\kappa$ ($0.45$--$0.74$); the gap intensifies with model capability ($r = 0.23$, $p \\leq 0.001$ for Qwen vs.\\ $r \\approx 0$ for Mistral/LLaMA), showing reasoning homogeneity is architecture-independent (\\S\\ref{sec:process-kappa}).\n38\t\n39\t\\item \\textbf{Regime-Dependent Diagnostics.} Three failure modes create regime boundaries; $C_0$ combined with SC accuracy provides a zero-cost diagnostic predicting when symbolic augmentation is unlikely to help (\\S\\ref{sec:main-results}).\n40\t\\end{enumerate}"
  },

    "snippet": "1\tSelf-Consistency (SC) majority voting over chain-of-thought traces saturates as sample count grows because same-model traces are correlated voters.\n2\tWe investigate whether symbolic constraint extraction can break this plateau through Symbolic Invariant Consensus Aggregation (SICA), which extracts first-order logic (FOL) constraints from reasoning traces and selects answers via weighted partial MAX-SAT.\n3\tEvaluating six models (7B--32B) across three reasoning domains with over 3,000 condition-evaluations (${\\sim}$1,250 unique instances), we find that self-extracted constraints fail to surpass majority voting: none of fifteen non-degenerate conditions shows a significant positive effect ($p < 0.05$), while two conditions show significant degradation.\n4\tWe identify \\emph{symbolic confirmation bias} as the root cause: on incorrect problems, 83.3\\% of self-extracted constraints support the wrong majority answer (bias ratio $4.97\\times$), and 94\\% of errors overlap with SC.\n5\tProcess-level analysis reveals a deeper bottleneck: chain-of-thought cosine similarity ($0.69$) far exceeds answer-level Fleiss' $\\kappa$ ($0.41$), indicating that traces share reasoning processes even when they diverge on answers.\n6\tMore than twenty same-model remediation strategies spanning fifteen distinct families---from prompt engineering through method-level redesign to ten additional directions---all fail, revealing a persistent independence-accuracy trade-off at the 7B--32B scale: methods that increase trace independence destroy accuracy (Debate: $\\kappa = 0.15$, $-18.6$\\,pp), while methods preserving accuracy cannot break the correlation floor ($\\kappa \\geq 0.40$ across three model families).\n7\tOracle controls confirm the aggregation mechanism is sound ($+29.90$\\,pp and $+14.22$\\,pp on FOLIO, $+11.50$\\,pp ProofWriter; $p < 0.001$); same-model self-extraction captures only ${\\sim}$12\\% of the oracle ceiling.\n8\tHowever, a cross-architecture proof-of-concept---DeBERTa-large NLI combined with SC---achieves $+3.43$\\,pp ($p = 0.039$, McNemar exact test) through per-label complementarity, demonstrating that the oracle gap is practically recoverable when verification signal comes from outside the reasoning model's distribution."
  },

24	DeBERTa NLI alone achieves 49.51\%, below SC's 54.41\% baseline. Combo mode at $w{=}1$ (NLI as 13th voter) reaches 57.84\% ($\Delta = +3.43$\,pp, McNemar exact $p = 0.039$; discordant pairs: $b{=}1$, $c{=}8$; 95\% bootstrap CI $[0.59, 6.28]$\,pp). The effect increases with weight: $w{=}3$ yields 58.82\% ($+4.41$\,pp, $p = 0.078$, n.s.; $b{=}6$, $c{=}15$; CI $[0.00, 8.82]$\,pp) and $w{=}5$ reaches 60.29\% ($+5.88$\,pp); neither reaches significance. Higher weights risk dataset-specific overfitting; we report $w{=}1$ as the primary result. Note that this cross-architecture approach operates outside the same-model self-extraction scope of SICA (\S\ref{sec:pipeline}), serving as a proof-of-concept for external signal integration.
```

```json
{
  "parentUuid": "54ddf192-8b01-4358-b034-ae5684805e51",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "attachment": {
    "type": "edited_text_file",
    "filename": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0513_3/projects/symb_invariant_consensus/docs/paper/solutions.tex",
    "snippet": "1\t\\section{Breaking Confirmation Bias}\n2\t\\label{sec:solutions}\n3\t\n4\tThe analyses in \\S\\ref{sec:confirmation-bias}--\\S\\ref{sec:remediation} identify two structural causes of SICA's failure: constraints are coupled to their source answer (confirmation bias), and all traces share the same model distribution (independence bottleneck). We investigate two complementary approaches: decoupling constraints from answers within the same model (\\S\\ref{sec:decoupling}), and injecting an architecturally independent verification signal (\\S\\ref{sec:cross-arch}).\n5\t\n6\t\\subsection{Constraint-Answer Decoupling}\n7\t\\label{sec:decoupling}\n8\t\n9\tSymbolic confirmation bias arises because the model generates an answer, then extracts constraints consistent with it (\\S\\ref{sec:confirmation-bias}). The causal chain runs through the trace conclusion: the final answer anchors extraction toward confirming that specific answer.\n10\t\n11\tWe break this chain by truncating the conclusion from each reasoning trace before constraint extraction. The final answer segment (${\\sim}$4.1\\% of trace text) is removed so the extraction model sees only intermediate reasoning steps, not the concluded answer. All other pipeline components---deduplication, MAX-SAT solving, scoring---remain identical to standard SICA (\\S\\ref{sec:pipeline}).\n12\t\n13\tOn Mistral-7B FOLIO ($n = 204$), truncated-SICA shows a declining trajectory as evaluation progresses: $\\Delta = +11.3$\\,pp at $n = 70$ (early, easy-problem bias) $\\to$ $+3.1$\\,pp at $n = 120$ $\\to$ $+1.3$\\,pp at $n = 140$ $\\to$ $+0.6$\\,pp at $n = 180$. The near-complete result ($+0.6$\\,pp, n.s.) indicates that removing the answer segment is insufficient: the intermediate reasoning steps themselves carry enough answer signal to reconstruct confirmation bias during extraction.\n14\t\n15\t\\subsection{Cross-Architecture NLI Verification}\n16\t\\label{sec:cross-arch}\n17\t\n18\tThe oracle gap (\\S\\ref{sec:oracle}) shows that the MAX-SAT mechanism is sound given quality constraints; twenty-plus same-model strategies fail to provide them (\\S\\ref{sec:remediation}). The corrective signal must come from outside the reasoning model's distribution.\n19\t\n20\t\\paragraph{Method.}\n21\tWe test this with DeBERTa-large \\citep{he2021deberta} (406M parameters, encoder-only), fine-tuned on MultiNLI \\citep{williams2018mnli}. For each problem, DeBERTa classifies the (premise, conclusion) pair into \\textsc{entailment}, \\textsc{contradiction}, or \\textsc{neutral}. In combo mode, the NLI verdict is integrated with SC's $K$ votes via a weight parameter $w$: each NLI-supporting vote counts $w$ times. We evaluate on Mistral-7B FOLIO ($n = 204$).\n22\t\n23\t\\paragraph{Main result.}\n24\tDeBERTa NLI alone achieves 49.51\\%, below SC's 54.41\\% baseline. Combo mode at $w{=}1$ (NLI as 13th voter) reaches 57.84\\% ($\\Delta = +3.43$\\,pp, McNemar exact $p = 0.039$; discordant pairs: $b{=}1$, $c{=}8$; 95\\% bootstrap CI $[0.59, 6.28]$\\,pp). The effect increases with weight: $w{=}3$ yields 58.82\\% ($+4.41$\\,pp, $p = 0.078$, n.s.; $b{=}6$, $c{=}15$; CI $[0.00, 8.82]$\\,pp) and $w{=}5$ reaches 60.29\\% ($+5.88$\\,pp); neither reaches significance. Higher weights risk dataset-specific overfitting; we report $w{=}1$ as the primary result. Note that this cross-architecture approach operates outside the same-model self-extraction scope of SICA (\\S\\ref{sec:pipeline}), serving as a proof-of-concept for external signal integration.\n25\t\n26\t\\paragraph{Per-label complementarity.}\n27\tThe gain is not uniform across labels. On \\textsc{False} problems, NLI achieves 71.43\\% compared to SC's 38.10\\% ($+33$\\,pp), because DeBERTa's contradiction detection directly identifies negated conclusions that the LLM's chain-of-thought reasoning systematically mishandles. On \\textsc{Unknown} problems, the pattern reverses: SC reaches 68.12\\% while NLI drops to 18.84\\% ($+49$\\,pp for SC), because ``neutral'' in NLI does not map cleanly to the logical \\textsc{Unknown} label. The combo succeeds because these strengths are complementary. When both systems agree (45.1\\% of problems), accuracy reaches 68.48\\% ($+14.07$\\,pp over SC), confirming that agreement between architecturally independent systems serves as a high-precision signal.\n28\t\n29\t\\paragraph{Why cross-architecture verification works.}\n30\tThe 406M encoder-only model and the 7B decoder-only LLM share no architecture, training data, or inference mechanism. DeBERTa classifies textual entailment rather than generating chain-of-thought, so it does not exhibit the symbolic confirmation bias identified in \\S\\ref{sec:confirmation-bias}. The two systems err on different problem subsets, satisfying the independence condition that same-model sampling cannot provide."
  },

### Tool result

_source line 11908 · 2026-05-20T13:41:16.209Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01DnpFhUqjWGP2vPZYd8h3RW`</summary>

```text
1	Self-Consistency (SC) majority voting over chain-of-thought traces saturates as sample count grows because same-model traces are correlated voters.
2	We investigate whether symbolic constraint extraction can break this plateau through Symbolic Invariant Consensus Aggregation (SICA), which extracts first-order logic (FOL) constraints from reasoning traces and selects answers via weighted partial MAX-SAT.
3	Evaluating six models (7B--32B) across three reasoning domains with over 3,000 condition-evaluations (${\sim}$1,250 unique instances), we find that self-extracted constraints fail to surpass majority voting: none of fifteen non-degenerate conditions shows a significant positive effect ($p < 0.05$), while two conditions show significant degradation.
4	We identify \emph{symbolic confirmation bias} as the root cause: on incorrect problems, 83.3\% of self-extracted constraints support the wrong majority answer (bias ratio $4.97\times$), and 94\% of errors overlap with SC.
5	Process-level analysis reveals a deeper bottleneck: chain-of-thought cosine similarity ($0.69$) far exceeds answer-level Fleiss' $\kappa$ ($0.41$), indicating that traces share reasoning processes even when they diverge on answers.
6	More than twenty same-model remediation strategies spanning fifteen distinct families---from prompt engineering through method-level redesign to ten additional directions---all fail, revealing a persistent independence-accuracy trade-off at the 7B--32B scale: methods that increase trace independence destroy accuracy (Debate: $\kappa = 0.15$, $-18.6$\,pp), while methods preserving accuracy cannot break the correlation floor ($\kappa \geq 0.40$ across three model families).
7	Oracle controls confirm the aggregation mechanism is sound ($+29.90$\,pp and $+14.22$\,pp on FOLIO, $+11.50$\,pp ProofWriter; $p < 0.001$); same-model self-extraction captures only ${\sim}$12\% of the oracle ceiling.
8	However, a cross-architecture proof-of-concept---DeBERTa-large NLI combined with SC---achieves $+3.43$\,pp ($p = 0.039$, McNemar exact test) through per-label complementarity, demonstrating that the oracle gap is practically recoverable when verification signal comes from outside the reasoning model's distribution.
9	
```

29	A cross-architecture proof-of-concept demonstrates that this gap is recoverable: DeBERTa-large NLI combined with SC achieves $+3.43$\,pp ($p = 0.039$, McNemar exact test) through per-label complementarity (\S\ref{sec:cross-arch}).
30	
31	Our contributions are:
32	\begin{enumerate}[nosep,leftmargin=*]
33	\item \textbf{Independence-Accuracy Trade-off and Cross-Architecture Escape.} Fleiss' $\kappa$ across four aggregation methods and three model families reveals a same-model Pareto frontier ($\kappa \geq 0.40$). Twenty-plus remediation strategies spanning fifteen families fail; a cross-architecture NLI verifier (DeBERTa-large, $+3.43$\,pp, $p = 0.039$) demonstrates the frontier is escapable (\S\ref{sec:remediation}, \S\ref{sec:cross-arch}).

```text
23	\paragraph{Main result.}
24	DeBERTa NLI alone achieves 49.51\%, below SC's 54.41\% baseline. Combo mode at $w{=}1$ (NLI as 13th voter) reaches 57.84\% ($\Delta = +3.43$\,pp, McNemar exact $p = 0.039$; discordant pairs: $b{=}1$, $c{=}8$; 95\% bootstrap CI $[0.59, 6.28]$\,pp). The effect increases with weight: $w{=}3$ yields 58.82\% ($+4.41$\,pp, $p = 0.078$, n.s.; $b{=}6$, $c{=}15$; CI $[0.00, 8.82]$\,pp) and $w{=}5$ reaches 60.29\% ($+5.88$\,pp); neither reaches significance. Higher weights risk dataset-specific overfitting; we report $w{=}1$ as the primary result. Note that this cross-architecture approach operates outside the same-model self-extraction scope of SICA (\S\ref{sec:pipeline}), serving as a proof-of-concept for external signal integration.
```

</details>
